"WOMEN'S CHAT!"

Over everything there is the shadew of the war but the suggestion that there should be a " day of humili- ation "early in the New Year. has been very properly put aside as altogether °pressed to the national senti- ment. Lord Aberdeen mad.; a similar proposition in 1854, but the Queen was of the opinion that if any fieople had cause to feel humiliated it was not the British nation. So also on this occasion Her Majesty t: kes a much more sensible view of the situation. The Queen desires, and has appointed Sunday the 7th of January for a simultaeeeus collection in the churches throughout England and Wales for the benefi7 of our soldiers and sailors and their families. The appeal was anticipated in many churches by the sc-called Soldiers' Sueday, on November 19tn, but the supplication now assumes a much more national character, and no doubt the moo- - meat will extend to the Catholic and Congregational Churches all over the Kingdom. All the Churches are on common ground in this helpful work. It is really an appeal to all, and the result should be a vast addition to the funds for the aufferers from the war in South Africa. The death of the Duke of Westminster, who was born in 1825, adds another to the long list of distinguished people who have passed away in 1899. Though the Grosvenor family is one of the oldest if the aristocracy the deceased nobleman was the first Duke, and he was of course wealthy beyond the dreams of avarice. The Westminster estates include 30,000 acres in Cheshire and Flintshire, and over 600 in the most fashionable district of London. It is from the latter property that the Grosvenors derive the bulk of their wealth. The late Duke always recognised:the responsibility which attached to his vast possessions, and was a generous though always a careful dispenser of charity. Last July his eldest grand-daughter was married to the Earl of Shaftesbury, and it was at the Earl's seat at St. Giles in Dorset, that the Duke passed away. It is Lady Shaftesbury's younger brother, Viscount Belgrave, son of the late Earl Grosvenor who died about 15 yeas ago, who now succeeds to the Dukedom. Lord Belgrave is in his twenty-first year, and has been serving as aide- de-camp to Sir Alfred Milner at the Cape. It was on Mr. Gladstone's recommendation that the late owner of Eaton Hall was raised from a Marquis to the Dukedom, in 1874, and it was under imilar circum- stances that he received the Garter, which is now at the diiipasal of the Queen. The old friendship, and long political association between the Duke aro' leir Glad- stone, was sadly broken over the Irish quceition, estrangement was so bitter that it was Pile euejeer of common remark. It was not until 1894 that a complete , reconciliation took place between the riohe and Mr. Gladstoae, when the old affectionate ' w3e maintained until the end. Discussing wi..4 L. Guinci-. Rogers how greatly his old friends had fallen away from him on the Home Rule queition.Mr. Gladstone once said. " Of all my former colleagues who have left me, there is only one with whom I remain on the old terms, and it will perhaps be a surprise to you to learn who that is. There is not a stouter Unionist among them all, but we continue friends. It is the Duke of Argyle." The late Duke of Westminster was twice married, in 1852 to Lady Constance Leverson-Gower, and again in 1882 to Lady Catherine Cavendish. The second alliance created relationships calculated to baffle the most prac- treed genealogist, the Duke's second daughter having already married the new Duchess's brother. 'l6e Duchess has ever been deemed an ideal hostess, as was her predecessor, the brilliant Lady Constance, but it is an open secret that her real happiness has been found in quiet domestic life. ohe is simplicity itself, and is much beloved by her large circle of friends. New Year's Day has always been memorable for presents. In the reign of Elizabeth, the greater part of the peers and peeresses, all the bishops, the chief officers of state, and several of the household servants gave New Year's gifts to her Majesty. The Queen gave gifts also, on her side, but the balance was always carefully made much in her favour. Pins, then, very dear, were acceptable, gloves then very costly, were also general presents. As a solemn religious festival, the first day of the year seems to have been celebrated by the most ancient nations. By the Jew as well as the Mohammedan, by the Chinese, the Roman and the Egyptian, it was marked by the observance Of particular ceremonies. The early fathers, Chrysacton, Ambrose, and others, forbade the celebration of any festivities on this anniversary, as a relic of paganism, but the joyous character of the day was never wholly ignored. -tle item the smart woman never loses sight of is —seise edges ! more especially those of tailor build. Once a skirt of this description has to be turned up,and there!,, re shortened, because the edge is cut through, the skir: is uined. Nothing is so dowdy as a skirt too alaort . Illy decent seamstress can fix a braid, but the r:rue person if she be entrusted with altering the edge Neil probably make a hash of the whole affair. By we' oliins, the edge we prevent all the necessity for e;eroas interference. Sel,s are in full swing, and women are succumbing in their thousands every day. The bulk of the "bargains" seem to bear the impress of the fogs that have been all too numerous this winter, and purchasers would do well to see those items se ePe.d, in a 7 light. Of course there really are some bargainr to e had among the heaps of rubbish brc ught forward at h%le time,but there is very little doubt that those particular functions are more in favour ef tle3 shop i_.-,reerietors than their fair customPra. During the past two cr three yen, there has been a considerable improvement instrle see %gilt of our clothes. Whereas we all used to wear three or four underskirts we are now relieved of all tut on 3. or at most two. The result is, we can v. s.lk further and quicker than of yore, when encumbered with a inas of endergarments that twisted round our limbs and obliged us every now and then to shake ourselves almost to a minimum, and we can walk, run, and enjoy outdoor life much better than we cculd ever do before. The death of Mr. Moody recalls the great stir which he and Mr Sankey creatsd in the country twenty-five years ago. When they arrived in 1873, and began their two years mislion over here, in Glasgow, few people had beard of them, but gradually their audiences grew until there were no buildings large enough to hold the crowds that flocked to hear them. No one, not even Mr. Spur- geon, Mr. Gladstone, or Mr. Bright could attract such multitudes,and yet there was apparently nothing except earnestness in the two American ministers to account for their popularity. Mr. Moody was neither a cultured man, or an orator, and there was nothing exceptional in Mr. Sankey's sing- ing. There arc scores and hundreds of such men in tbe Salvation Army, but the Moody and Sankey revival services seemed to fill a want of the time, and thus be- came a popular craze. Mr. Moody undoubtedly did a great deal of'good. He received large sums of money from the public, which he devoted to good services. He frequently took between two and three thousands pounds weekly, in the collections, at his religious services, and the royalties on the Moody and Sankey hymn book are said to have brought in between three and four hundred thousand pounds. AAADIES' LETTER.

MT DWI NELLIB

Evening dresses are in great request just now, for parties art, ever associated with Yuletide. A charming but at the same time thoroughly useful dress for a dance is made of one of the popular figured black nets mounted over rather bright rose. pink astin of the cotton backed variety, this being more effective than sateen ank less expensive than glacee silk, although the latter is, of course, better for the purpose. The net skirt is edged with five little frilis, each bordered with a row of block velvet baby-ribbon, and is just) eased into the waistband round front and sides and gath.red a little at the back, the satin slip being made sepa- rately and trimmed with a foot-frill. The bodice is full, the top being outlined with two or three narrow, velvet-edged frills, while the high chemi- sette and sleeves are of transparent black lace. A sash of rose-red silk, tied at the side of the front ;with long, deeply-fringed ends, and a big cluster of roses on the left shoulder, complete a very smart but not too oostly gown, or a waist band of ribbon zan be worn with a pretty bow on the left side. The long white glove, where the bodior has no fieeves, are absolutely necessary.

Blouses tor evening wear are coming to the front just now, and they are to be seen in all kinds of coloured silk, etc. For the more expensive type of dress, glace silk and a thick, soft satin are the two most popular materials, setticg the very fashionable but very terribly costly pinne aside. A very stylish gown of delicate heliotrope silk has the skirt made with a siraulated tunic out into deep scallops and edged with a ruching of chiffon in a slightly lighter shade. There are many arrangements to be suggested for the bodice. I have already seen several. One of them, for example, was of black chenille-spotted chiffon with collar and shoulder-straps of jet embroidery, and the other cf black and rose striped glace, trimmed with very tiny frills of black gauze, and finished at the waist with a sash of rose-coloured ribbon.

That fur is to be the order for milinery cannot be doubted seeing how widespread is its adoption. Of course a combination of ribbon is absolutely necessary, whilst the effact of a smartly trimrnal hats is considerably enhanced by an airette. Amongst recent ohapaoux that I have seen was a smut little toque with black velvet crown and border of erminit, with supplements of black vel- vet and ostrich tips; en suite wiLh a muff of ermine, with fron-frou of lace emerging from its cosy depths each end, accompany this coat very effectively. The second hat showed the wide toque of this season, designed to outline the semi-circle of the coiffure, The brim was sable, and the trimming consisted of bright rose velvet and grey, speckled feathers sewn with gold and silver sequins. The third hat was in mouse-coloured velvet lined with orange silk arranged in tucks. Folds of white chiffon surround the crown and a rosette of the same rests upon the hair under the black feather which is fastened °tithe turned-up part of the brim.

Jnet now children's clothes are interesting, espe. daily those for small girls, as the colotria in vogue for pilistse?and coats, trocks and hats are cilarmi.44, letots are being worn by girls up to some twelve year? old, made generally sac-fitting, often of a pow.ier or gobelin blue, old rose or art green shade. Tneee paletots have fano:fat collars, either of the same material, or of velvet, or again of the material embroidered by hand in crewel silk, a tone lighter than '..he material. avich bonnets ot the material of the ps.leto: turned biek with an embroidered rever, can be worn up to six or seven. Alter that age a hat is primps better. CHAPTER XIV.

When Alec came to himself he could not at first remember what had happened to him. He felt chilled and stiff in every joint. There was hardly a glimmer of light in the place where he found himself. Where was he? At last he remembered be had had an accident, and he had fallen from— no, it was not possible! Then, all at once, just as a light is flashed in a dark cellar, the truth flashed across him. It was as if full daylight instead of darkness enveloped all the events of the last few days. He had been pursued and trapped by his enemies, and these enemies were the Feltons! He tried to rise but was soon obliged to desist, so much pain did any movement make him suffer. How blind he had been! Of course, Bob's friends —that is, those he had named—were the men of this house, and Bb had been unable or unwilling to tell him. Having been foiled in one instance, they had tried again. But why had the squire asked him to Yewdale? Surely not for this! Surely he knew nothing about it! No, Alec refused to believe this. It must have been the Feltons. But what could be their motive? He had always said that he did not count upon this property. Let the squire marry again and have a son, then Alec would have no chance of coming into a penny of it. Older men than the squire married every day. Why should the Feltons hate him as a possible heir? Was their reason, Ruby? But till his arrival at Yewdale, he had never thought of Ruby. They could not have told by intuition that he would fall in love at first sight with her. No, that was impossible. Bob had warned him to go away, but even if he had ex- pta.ined himself more, how could he have guessed or believed in so much treachery? Besides, would he not have run any risk for Ruby's sake? Now, however, he clenched his teeth. He was here, %!'iierever that was, and could do nothing. He - -Ad not save her. Would that wretch, James Felton, take her away, and would no one be there to save her?

Then Alec again tried to rise. This time he felt stronger. He passed his hand down his leg. He had not broken any bones, but he was terribly braised. Then suddenly he remembered that he had a box of matches in his pocket. He felt for it and struck a light. The match spluttered for a moment, then shone brightly over the walls of his prison. He saw that he was in a sort of lumber- room. There were old carpets and old curtains on the floor, and this fact must have saved his life. It felt very damp and cold, but the one window the room possessed was barred, and evidently looked only into another room. Indeed, after striking another match, he came to the conclusion that he was in a sort of closet where for many years rubbish had been thrown, and that there was no communication with the outer world. Yes, that was most likely the fact. He was in an inner chamber or closet, and that, if the room with which it communicated was also locked, then most likely no one would hear him, however loud he might shout. He was meant to starve or to die in any fashion which seemed best to him.

But why? Why? To die fighting is comparatively easy. To die uhen overwhelmed by some great misfortune, is not so terrible as some might think. One can but struggle the few minutes allotted to one; but to die in a trap, like a wild animal, to be able to do nothing towards saving one's own life, that it is which takes away a proud man's courage, and makes the strongest man to quail. Before his second match went out, Alec's eves eagerly searched around to see how the dastardly plot had been contrived. The secret was well kept; nothing was visible. Above was the unceiled wood- work, and the trap-door was so contrived that the marks of it were not discernible. It must have been cleverly made, or it must have been there a long time. Which? He was meant to starve, for there was no sign of food—not a vestige of anything eatable—nothing but lumber. Presently the returning light streamed in a little, and when he began to get accustomed to the semi-darkness, he could see plainly that the doer wa: also firmly locked from the outside. This was no mere common mcdern woodwork, but strong oak, studded with nails. Where was his chance of escape? But a man of Alec's character does not easily give up. He stood up and looked round carefully, thankful at last that no bones were broken, though his left wrist was very painful, and he fancied that he must have sprained it severely. Hero was another hindrance to his escape. Very slowly be went round the place and examined his small prison. lii case corner there were pieture- frames, old chairs, orse or two travatling-boxes, and the odds and ends of year.s. Cobwebs everywhere on the walls, and the window with thick ground-glass, covered with a shutter on the outside. The light came through the crevices where the shutter did not fit very tightly. He thumped the walls ;.they were so thick that they hardly made an echo. He shouted, and the sound dropped dull and heavy, as if it were smothered before it was out of his mouth. _ _ _

"lam a prisoner, and it is certain that I am to be kept here—how long ? " He clenched his teeth as he sank down upon the old heap of damp curtains and wrestled with thought. It was hard for a man who had done no harm to his neighbour, and who had all his life been free from ideas of evil, to find himself suddenly confronted with villainy of which he cannot understand the reason, and for which there is no apparent motive. Alec tried to go over all that had happened to him since he had received that fatal invitation to come to Yewdale. His accident—which he saw now was no accident, but planned to get rid of him, and which might have suceeeded had Bob not been at hand—then the squire's request to make Ruby his heir, then his own happiness or misfortune in falling in love with the girl who must be a victim—or could she be also in the secret? Was all this a plant to draw him into worse meshes?

He flung his head back indignantly. "How dare I think such a thing of her? No, she is as much in the meshes of those men as I am. But what of her father? How could it be possible that he should sacrifice her from any motive? How could he use her for his own advantage? It was too mysterious; he could nut see any daylight. "Ruby," he said, softly, "what will you think Of me? Ah, that seems the hardest part of it! You will be waiting, and there will be no Alec to help you, and you had trusted me. Will they take her now? My poor little bird, you have been in a cage all these years, and you little guess that I am at this moment in the same and worse straits. No, not worse. You will be in the power of that wretch, and I can but die. But die, like this— like this! No, it is impossible."* But much as he might think, there was nothity, to be done—nothing. The minutes passed away like hours, and the hours like years, and there was only silence. At last, tired even, of expecting some sound, he rose, and again he went round the room. Again he hit the cold glass with all his might, and again there was no response bat silence. It was maddenning, and even now what might not be happening to Ruby? What? Never had Alec experienced such a feeling of hatred and rage as seized him at this moment. It was the impotence of his anger which was more humiliating than anything else. He felt that he was indulging in an anger which only recoiled on himself. Not a righteous anger, but one which seemed to lower his own bbaracter. Suddenly his better nature reasserted itself. "I am in God's hands. What can I do? But -I am pot forsaken. If this thing is to be, it must be God's will. I have but to bear it like a man—not like a beast who cannot look up." It was a hard lesson, harder because he thought of Ruby before himself. Why had he not tatei her away at once when he understood the true meaning of all these machinations? Why? Why? At last he fell into a troubled sleep, for throug;: it he seemed conscious of his position. It was as if someone had given him an opiate for his mental pain. Through this he heard a faint—very faint— noise. It was some human being; at all events, hu should see someone. He was just going to tend up and shout, when a sudden thought made him lie where he was perfectly still. It could be but the person or persons who had put him there who were coming to see him. Perhaps to gloat over his death. No, he would not be the first to speak. He would not give them the joy of wit- nessing his useless misery. He would give them silence for all comfort. Ile would be a man.

There was a faint footstep. It was much darker now. It must be getting late. He had forgotten to wind his watch, and Snow it had stopped. The

As he lifted his left hand, he was reminded of his sprain. How could he with one hand—and so bruised that he could hardly move—how could he hope to overpower James Felton, who was as tall as himself and as strong, if not stronger? The door opened very gently, and James Felton entered. "Forgive my intrusion, but I am going to- morrow, and this is my last chance of visiting a Beauworth."

Alec did not answer. Indeed, at that moment he could not have controlled himself to speak. "You have had an unpleasant adventure, but I am sorry to say it was necessary. You were making yourself very objectionable about • Ruby. You knew very well that she was to be my wife, and you meddled too much in that little matter." "What do, you want me to do ? " said Alec, unable to keep silent. "You are here because you wished to do too much."

Alec wanted to fall upon him. Words seemed inadequate; he paused to choose them. What could he say? He was too proud to aqk for pity. "I am going to-morrow. Have you any last word ? " continued James, but his tone was a little less assured.

"You are a dastardly coward!" said Alec, slowly, as if he were making an ordinary remark. James laughed softly. "Coward! No, I have not been oftep. 'old that; but this was a desperate remedy. It was the squire's fault. He wanted too much, and you chose to fall in love." ..

He could not lower himself to promise what he could never keep. After all he had better have said nothing. He saw now that he would be left hole, and— He did not dare face the result. • the key was turned in the lock. There was another sound, another grating noise and all pas stin again. Alec remained standing where he was as ii turned' to stone. What a fool he had been, why had he not promised, why had he given up so easily his chance of life? It was too trying to feel that he had been outwitted. Then suddenly Alec had another illumination, which comes to those who have to face death. Whether he had promised or not, he would have been left to die there. He even laughed a little as the certainty forced itself on him. He had not given his enemy the pleasure of a useless promise. If it had been within his plan to release him, he would not have taken such pains to have put him into this awful hole. Did anyone know? Alec believed that no one but, perhaps, James's father knew of his imprisonment. No one would look for him; no one would open this door, it might be for years, and he would die of slow starvation inch by inch. He threw himself down again on his carpets. There was nothing to do but to bear it like a man, 'nothing to hope but that Ged would•be merciful to him, and hasten the inevitable end. Prayer had not meant to Alec what it did now. It had never seemed to him that a man might at last find himself face to face wjth death, which is face to face with his Maker. A long silence followed, then there came a quiet determination to be a true man and a Christian, to die like a hero. He had nothing to reproach himself with, he had not to feel lower in his own estimation. He had loved Ruby, but not till he saw that it would save her from those wretches had he attempted to inter- fere. Poor, poor Ruby, he had taught her what true love meant only to make her fate doubly bitter, but even now he did not repent, though the idea that she would belong to another, and such another, was maddening. Still it was better for her to know wrong from right, even if her sufferings were to be doubled. What would slow starvation feel like? This thought came upon him later on. It seemed to him that any death would be preferable, but there wasjno choice given to him, and again he searched for some adequate reason for this monstrous injustice to an unoffending man. His mind went back to his childhood, to the time when his adopted mother had first taken care of him, after he had been left friendless and motherless. Since that time he had never wanted for affec- tion, or for anything which she had been able to give him. Happily this second mother was dead, and in her wildest imagination she could never have foreseen his fate, nor the awful death which awaited him. _ _

The day wore on night fell. It must be getting late. He remembered stories of men who had lived fin- days without food, unable to part with life which they valued so little. His mind seemed to grow clearer instead of becoming clouded. Why was he to suffer in this way? Even his bruises were less painful. He stood up and felt that he could walk better in the narrow space allotted to him, but where was the good of trying to get more energy when the case was hopeless? Night came on—night, with all the added feeling that this was Ruby's last night in her old home. He struck his fist against the wall, as if this could release him from prison, and as if the very walls must take pity on him and on her. There could be no escape for her, no more than there could be for him, and her prison would end in worse than death. The best thing to hope for her was neglect, for there could be nothing in common between the refined, beautiful girl and the man capable of any crime to get his own miseraule ends.

Alec felt he must get weary so as to court sleep, that only benefactor left to those forsaken by men. God alone could help him now. For what sin was he placed here in this terrible strait? For what sin ?

Then his mind reverted to Bob, kind Bob, who had seen more and knew more than he had revealed. But even if he had told him, would he have believed him? Would he not have thought that the lad was trying to shield his own friends at the expense of Alec's relations?

Bob was a sharp lad, but what chance was there that he would find out about his disappearance? James was clever enough to have removed all tracf, of his fall, ge.wonl4 invent some plausible stgry

to pat Bch off the scent. There was no hope, no chance for him.

None, none, none. As he repeated the words he seemed to be falling very gently down, as if the wings of a great bird, floating down, down into soft blue mist followed by a beautiful oblivion.

"If you will come into the office, madam, you can see the directory." Alas! poor Ruby ; she saw a long list of Scotts. She looked down. First she thought that one was the right one, then she saw another, and was fairly puzzled. A feeling of despair came over her. What should she do if she could not find this house? She wrote down two addresses, pretending she had found what she wanted, and then she went back to eat her lunch. She could hardly eat any- thing, and when she asked the price, she found it was three shillings and sixpence. As soon as she had paid, she fled from that big house, where there were no kind looks. She felt so lonely and desolate. What could she do? She must try and find the two Mrs. Scots. She would not lose the address this time. She had to ask several times for the way, and at last when she reached the house her heart beat fast. She rang the bell. "Was Mrs. Scott at home P" "Mrs. Scott was abroad till the summer," said a caretaker. Ruby walked away. Then it must be the other. Again she asked her way. Again she felt weary, and her heart sank within_her. At last she found the house. "Is Mrs. Scott at home ? " The servant of whom she asked the question was a young woman, who looked Ruby up and down as much as to say: "What do you want of Mrs. Scott ? " "Yes, will you walk in ? " Ruby walked upstairs to a drawing-room. She waited some time, her fears becoming more and more acute. At last she heard a rustle of skirts, and a lady sailed in. The sight of her took all Ruby's courage away. "Are you the Mrs. Scott who is the friend of Mr. Beauworth ? " "Mr. Beauworth ? Well, let me see. Do I know a gentleman of that name? I daresay Ido —I know so many." "Oh, but you sent him a telegram yesterday." "I am afraid it was not yesterday. But what can I do for you ? " "Oh, nothing, thank you. " "Oh, no, thank you; I have come up for the day, and I--" "Oh, very well; but if you are in need of a bed; I let apartments." Ruby thought that this was some ray of comfort. "What do you charge for a bed just for one night till I find Mrs. Scott ? " "Oh, not much for a nice-looking young lady as you are." "How much ? " said Ruby, in a low voice. She did not like the looks of this London lady, but she was in despair. "Let us say five shillings a night. suit you ? " "Thank you, I will come back this evening if I don't find Mrs. Scott."

"Very well; I am sure you could not do better. Leave your bag here, anyhow: it is heavy for you. Why not stay here altogether? You can write to your friend." Ruby refused to stay, hardly knowing what she said, she was so tired and so sad; but she left her baR in the hall. Would she have to go home after all? She hardly had enough money to stay more than the night. Oh, how could her father have driven her to such straits? How could he? When Ruby found herself once more in the streets, she almost broke down. Her tears fell fast, but she was not beaten yet. She would go to a bookseller's shop and ask for another directory. An hour later Ruby was once more looking for Alec's friend, but it was like looking for the proverbial needle. She tried three. One was a shop, that could not be the place, and another was certainly not the required Mrs. Scott. At last Ruby felt quite wearied out. She must return to the first Mrs. Scott, who would give her a bed for the night, and after that—oh, she must think! She passed a church and went into it. Kneeling down, she acalted God to shew her some way out of her difficulty. She felt a little comforted after this, and retraced her steps. It was growing dusk. Her mind wandered back to her home, where so short a time ago she had tholght that she was so safe and happy and only a child! How quickly life could change! When she reached the street, she paused. She knew this house at 11 events, but she was unwilling to come in—only, what could she do?

CHAPTER XV.

The train was whirled along to its destination to the great centre of the life of the British Empire. The thought that converges and the stream of trade that flows to that centre, as if impelled by some hidden law which it is bound to obey, is one of the unexplained secrets of national life. The history of centuries has willed it, so a daily:miracle takes place and few realise it because it is a fact of everyday life. Ruby Beauworth could hardly remember going in a train since she was a child. And now she was alone in a corner of the third-class carriage. Her heart beat very fast. She had seen James Felton and his father, and the terror of being dis- covered by them seemed to take away all her power of thinking. Every now and then she said to herself I mifq escape. I won't go with him, I won't.

But if he saw her he would, perhaps, appeal to a policeman. He would say that she had run away, and poor Ruby, who knew very little of the law, conjured up all kinds of dreadful things that might happen. Oh, why had Alec left her? but it must be some mistake, he could not help it. He had been so good, so kind, that he would have come to her help if he could. She grasped the telegram tightly so that it might impart some degree of comfort to her. The lady had said that she would take her in. She was Alec's friend, and surely she would help her. The train stopped suddenly, Ruby dared not ask any questions. It was not London, but perhaps she would hear the name called out. She listened, then tried to look out, but at that minute she saw James Felton get out of the train and begin walking towards her carriage. Had he seen her or had someone told him that she was there quite near to him. He was smoking. She went into the furthest corner of the carriage, but at that moment her own door was opened and an elderly man got in. James was coming nearer, she could see him through her veil. At that moment, however, she heard the guard say: "Take your seats." James hurried back and she was safe. She heaved a little sigh of relief and squeezed herself still more into the corner. The man who had got into the carriage looked at her.

"Be you going to London, Miss?" "Yes," said Ruby, not daring to look at the speaker. "It be a bit foggy to-day, and it will be worse in Lunnun."

===== 0002601_18991229_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
Ruby had not thought of the weather at all.

"It is foggy," she ;aid. The man looked at her a little more closely. He could hardly see her face, but he saw a pretty, girlish figure. She was evidently a lady born. "You be young to travel alone," he said, kindly. Ruby blushed very_ red. She seemed to feel as if everybody knew that she was running away. The train hurried on, and Ruby became more and more nervous. At last the rows of houses appeared, the steam slackened, the whistle sounded, and they had reached London. Ruby let the people get out. She was so much afraid of meeting James Felton, that she did not at first hear her companion saying: "Do you want a cab; missy ? " "No, thank you," said Ruby, anxious not to be beholden to any stranger, and the old man got out. At last the guard came by, and looked in. "It's the terminus, miss. Do you want a cab ? " "Oh, yes, please." Ruby got down and took her telegram out of her pocket. There was no cab just near at hand. "Come this way, miss; I'll find you one in a minute." Ruby followed very closely. There seemed to be some protection in the official coat. The guard called a cab. Re opened the door. "Where to, miss? " _

Ruby gave the address. The guard shut tho door, and the cabman drove off. _

"Now I am safe," thought Ruby, lifting her veil from her face to cool her burning cheeks. "Now I shall escape him. 17 It was foggy here. The cab stopped at the entrance gates of the station. An official took town the number. Ruby looked out to wonder •.1-113, she was stopping, then the cab rumbled on into the streets. She did not know how long it would take her to get to her destination, so she sat at the edge of the seat and looked at the names of the streets. Every minute she felt happier and safer from him. Oh, if only the cab would get quialy to the address she had given. Suddenly, 'Wit liouf any warnmg",-- a hansonicab aprifthidned iier from the opposite side. Two men were in it. They looked full at her and she at them before Lee was time to move or to put down her veil. It was the Feltons, but their cab was dashing on; tad she saw James start, up and call the driver to st op them; the mist hid them the next moment. j 11 her resolution came to ker aid. "Please go very quickly," she called out, "and turn down this way, as I want to go by that street." She spoke with authority. The cabman was a stupid man, and obeyed as he -began to reckon up how much extra he might charge for going out of his way. "Go on quickly, please." Ruby heard the sound of wheels close behind her as her cab turned off from the thoroughfare. The other cab went on. They had not found out her cab had turned off. It was a long street, and her fourwheeler rumbled on.

"Go quicker, please," called out the girl. "I'm going as quick as the 'orse 'll go," said the driver. _ .

Ruby listened. Suddenly, as she gazed out, able saw a hansom turn into her street. He was follow- ing her. Again she put out her head. - "Stop, cabman, this is the place where 1 want to get out. Here is your money." She gave double the fare. She held her bag, and quick as thought she hurried down „a small side street, and dashed into a small shop. There she paused thinking and taking breath. Happily, no one was in the shop. It was .a very humble baker, and when he appeared Ruby asked for a bun and began to eat it, though every mouthful seemed to choke her. She kept her face away from the street, fearing to see her enemy following her. Before she had done, she heard a cab, and trembled for fear it was the cab, but it passed on. She must be safe. She took out her purse to pay for the bun, and then, for fear of exciting suspicion, she went out into the foggy air. With trembling steps she walked down the street, her mind almost stupefied with the fright she had had. What she must do next, she did not know; but the fact of walking did her good. The air cooled her burning forehead, and she was at last able to think coherently. She would call another cab, and go to the address of Alec's friend. Oh, now she was really safe. She had still a pound left; that would be enough to get her to a safe shelter till she could write to Alec. Oh, if he were only here! If she could see him suddenly appear round some corner, and hear his kind, strong voice tell her she was safe with him! But this would soon happen. His friend would know where he was. How fortunate that Bob had secured that telegram!

At last she got tired of walking, and paused to look at the address. She would learn it by heart to tell the cabman. Her hand was in her pocket/ she could feel nothing, she could—where was that paper? She stood quite still, feeling as if she should faint. She hunted again; then she remem- bered that she had held the envelope in her hand when she was in the cab. In her haste and agitation, she must have dropped it. Oh, where could it be? She was in this dreadful London; how could she find it? She did not even know the way she had come. She was lost—quite lost! She, who had never been allowed to wander out of the park alone, and now she felt utterly forsaken. She thought and thought. What could have been the address? She remembered the name—Mrs. Scott—and that it was in Kensington, but the street had flown from her memory. Some girls would have given way, but Ruby was a Beauworth. She stood a minute against the window of a shop pretending to look in, but seeing nothing with her bodily eyes. "I will go to an hotel, and then see if I can find this lady." She walked to where she saw several cabs stand- ing by the pavement. Ruby looked at the men; she chose the one who looked the oldest.

"Will you drive me to an hotel—a quiet one, please—in Kensington ? " "What name, miss ? " "I don't know, but one where a lady can stay, please." The old man eyed her a little, then he said: "All right," and rumbled off. When she reached the corner of the next street, she tried to read its name. Perhaps, if she saw it written up, she would remember. Nothing looked familiar to her. Nothing recalled the lost name. How long the cab went on! It seemed ages since she had got into it. Was the man in league with Janes Felton? All kinds of dreadful ideas surged

A WARRINGTON MINISTER IN AMERICA.

Testimonial to the Rev. Joseph Ellison. The many Warrington people who are acquainted with Councillor Luke Ellison and hi :3 family will be pleased to hear that his son Joseph, who has been Baptist minister at Carver, Mass, has recently accepted a call to Whittier, South Califoinia,where a new church has been built for him. The Rev. JBseph Ellison is highly esteemed in both these places, which are some 3,000 miles apart. We append a copy of a testimonial he has received on the occasion of his departure from Carver to Whittier.

The Plymouth County Neighbourhood Convention at its session in the Congregational 'Church, East Rochester, Mass., unanimously aaopted the follow- ing minute, April 11, 1899 :—Resolved, that this Convention place in the hand of the Rev, Joseph Ellison of the Baptist Church, Center Carver, Mass., certified by our Moderator and Secretary, as well as on our records, a statement of our high estimation of him as a thorough evangelical preacher of the Gospel, and our love for him as a Christian brother. We bear record to his fearless manner of pro- claiming the whole counsel of Gcd in his clear, forcible style, seeking the truth at a Sacrifice, and with unswerving loyalty to the Master. Anything that would dim the glory of the Son of God, or de- tract from the need and efficacy of his work 88 Saviour, has met with our brother's determined opposition. He has ever proclaimed the unlimited pardon for every sinner in Jesus Christ repenting. Such a course has won the high esteem of this Convention.

We regard our brother as an eminently safe teacher and guide, and would recommend him to any flock over which God shall place him as shepherd. We bear testimony not only to his faith- ful service in his distinctively pastoral duties, but also to his work for the past few years in helping with willing hands to build up a universal brother- hood, of one Faith, one Lord, one Baptism, in this neighbourhood, thus honouring the Master. There- fore he has endeared himself to us by a consecrated life of ability, faithfulness and love, which have characterised his labours among us. We bear glad witness to blessings received, heartseomforted,lives brightened and souls saved for the Kingdom by the Gospel so sweetly and touchingly sung by our brother—a rich gift freely used. We regret that this happy fellowship must be broken. We assure our brother and his family that our interest and prayers will follow them to the new field of work which shall be opened. We trust fora wider sphere for his usefulness,with a greater contribution to the glory of God. GEO. H. SHAW, Moderator. G. W. STEARNS, Secretary.

NOTlCE.—Solvers who become entitled to a prize in this compatition can, if they desire, have any book on the game of Draughts equal to the value of 58. instead of cash prize, by notifying the Draughts Editor.

In connection with the above competition we may state that we offer a monthly prize of 5s to the competitor who succeeds in placing himself at the head of the Solvers' List at the end of the month. Where the prize is awarded the winner's score is cancelled, while the remaining competitors' points go towards the next month', competition. The win- ner may re-enter the competition if be chooses commencing again at the bottom of the list. Points are awarded as follows: Two for the correct solu- tion of a problem, two for showing a dual solution, two for correcting the author's terms, and one for each minor correction.

Intenling competitors may commence at any time.

THE EXAMINER FRU) AY. DECEMBER 29, 1899. LEGISLATION IN 1899

NEWS OF TREAktE.

WARRINGTON' LIC A.GUE There have been no matches this week in the ye eague, but according to the fixtures arranged, good few will take place early in January. noubtless the players by that time will have suffi- ciently recovered from the general attacks of biliouaneQs, and other things, usually brought on about Christmas time. We can look forward to some very stiff struggles, both in the Ist and 2nd Division.. It is rather an unfortunate circumstance that the Y.M..C.A. should have been obliged to for- feit 4 points, through being unable to raise a team on two occasions,but of course the rules of the League must be duly recognized by one and all. There should be no partiality either for or against, and we are strongly of the opinion that the League will be much more of a success than it is now, when the Committee decide on the hard and fast rule, that is, should a club be unable to keep their engage-r.ent, no matter what the cause, they be compelled to forfeit the mate,' . f)f course, this will not meet with the approval of all the clubs in the League, but it would have the effect of stopping that postponing which was so prevalent last season among several of the clubs. We feel sure that if the committee adopted a rule to this effect, the benefits accruing therefrom would undoubtedly be felt both in the let and 2nd divisions.

vestries mtnus the women, and plus one new I power, that of attempting to deal with the terrible problem of ovtrcrowding. A slender interest, perhaps, attaches to the future fortunes of the new Borough or City of Westminster. Will it be able by the splendour of its cuisine to rival the Mansion House? But has not Whitechapel her whelks! A rivalry in guzzling may confidently be expected, and an interchange of hospitalities between the mayors of Shoreditch and Rotherhithe ought to lead to gastronomic results. The measure is not one which deserves serious treatment, though it may be regarded by enthusiasts as a step in the right direction.

THE MAYOR AND VACCINATION.

Discussion at the Police Court.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Tuesday morning, two applications were made by young men for vaccination exemption certificates. The magistrates were Mr. Birchall (in the chair), the Mayor. and Coun- cillors Smethurst and Shaw Green. The applicant in the first ca,,o was Walter James Birch, of Podgate-lane,who, when asked what his conscientious objection was,roplied that ho believed vaccination to be injurious to the health cf a child. Eln had seen children wno had been vaccin- ated who had been very poorly in health in consequence,.

The Mayor: What do you mean by being very poor.y? Applicant: Bad through being vaccinated.

Thc Mayor: For how long? - AppliPant : For a time. Tbm Mayor: You have to satisfy us that you know wh it you are talking about. rho Chairman : It would be a serious matter if we had a serious outbreak of small-polr. The Mayor: Do you kn3w anything about vaccin- ation at all?

===== 0002601_18991229_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
BY A. BIRRELL, Q.C., M.P

The nineteenth century rapidly approaches her bitter end. Wh will tie up her chin I nether know nor care. My admiration she has never had, though cheery must be that man's optimism who is persuaded that the twentieth century will be any improvement upon her. My only business is with the Statute Law of her last year, or (for I would avoid controversy) her last year but one. The Statutes 62 and 63 of the Queen make a slim volume—one of the slimmest of the reign. As I turn the pages with a listless eye, occasionally I stumble across a word or phrase reminiscent of a storm in the Par- liamentary tea-cup; but, for the most part, all is peaceful insignificance. Mr. Balfour's notion of a Bill is a short measure, with clauses so constructed as to avoid all known differences of opinion, and to prevent the points really at issue being in order.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HALF-TIM ERS.

The most important Statute of the year was a private member's Bill. This is so unusual a thing that it deserves commemoration. Everybody now knows how, at the -beginning of each session, private members ballot for places for private Bills. The members who draw the first half-dozen places have a chance of getting their Bills considered by the House. The rest may whistle, and let the world go by, for chance they have none. Last session Mr. Robson was the lucky man whose, name was the first to be drawn out of the bag. Here was his chance. What would he do with it? Luckily, the war in South Africa had not broken out, and so Mr. Robson was under no temptation to introduce a Bill for military conscription, or any other proud Imperialistic measure. He was content to stay at home, and espouse the cause of those unhappy youngsters whose hard, unchildish fate it is to divide their time between earning their livings arrd learning their lessons. Tha Act of 1893 had raised the age of total or partial exemp- tion from school-attendance from ten to eleven years; and now appeared the bold Mr. Robson with his ,Bill to clap on another year, and turn the eleven into twelve. What was the attitude of the Government? The poor thing having only a majonity of 150 or so, decided to do nothing, but to leave the House to make up its own mind. Unpopularity is one thing this Government never dare face. Over this year of child-life a furious faction fight arose. Factory owners love child-labour —it is cheap, and easily disciplined. Farmers still mourn over the good old times, when gangs of children, cruelly over-driven, toiled in their fields. Sportsmen find boy-beaters very handy at times. Parents, hard put to it for rent, are not averse to .a weekly con- tribution from their children. Even the Archbishops were somehow concerned in this question. Behind all these interests and powers there loomed large a by no means inconsider- abl3 party in the House of Commons who hate the education of the people, and are aver on the outlook for an opportunity to trip up the cause. Mr. Robson displayed great powers; he talked, and allowed himself to be talked to; he flattered bores, and conciliated inte- rests; he even besought interviews with the Archbishops. He has his reward. On New Year's Day his Bill, already an Act, will become the law of the laud. True it is, he had to make frien3s with the enemy and to accept provisoes which permit the law to be ovei-ridden and set at naught in certain cases. Nevertheless, Mr. Robson did well, and deserves the plaudit of a private member.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
SECONDARY EDUCATION.

The other educational measure of the year was a Government Bill after Mr. Balfour's own heart. It is entitled, "An Act to provide for the establishment of a Board of Education for England and Wales for matters connected therewith." The Board it establishes is, indeed, a Marvel. It is to consist of all the Secretaries of State for the time being, the First Lord of the Treasury, and the Chancellor of the Exchequer. The President of the Council is also to be a member; and, then, to give this party gang an air of indepen- dence, there is to be a President of the Board itself, who, though he is to be appointed by the Prime Minister for the time being, is to hold his office at the pleasure of the Crown. Sir John Gorst is to remain Vice-President of the Council, but he is to be the last Vice-President of the Council. He deserves to be. Never could we hope to see his like again. This impartial and well-informed Board of educational experts is to take the place of the Education Department, and of South Kensington, and of the Charity Com- missioners, except in their purely legal work. How it is to perform these multifarious duties we are not told, nor is a hint given. One has only to read the carefully-constructed and elaborately-designed statutes under which the Charity Commissioneas have hitherto per- formed their useful and difficult work to see how almost impossible it is to fit their pro- visions to an entirely altered state of circum- stances.

More shadowy legislation it is hard to imagine. Nothing is really dealt with. Everything is left to the Executive for the time being. This precious Board may inspect any Secondary school which cares to invite its inspection; and in order to supplement its vast ignorance her Majesty may, in the depth of her compassion, establish a Consultative Committee of Experts for the purpose of framing regulations for a register of teachers, and for advising the Board on any matter referred to it by the Board.

Haphazard legislation of this description is a simple negation by the House of Commons of its responsibilities. The Church party, always uneasy about the future and dreaming of a Radical Board of Education, protested with their accustomed vehemence, but in vain. Sufficient for Mr. Balfour's day is the evil thereof. There is peace in the grave.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LONDON ACT.

This is a measure of unutterable pomposity, and the smallest real significance. However, it contains thirty-five clauses, and calls into some sort of existence nearly as many boroughs; which are to have Mayor i and Aldermen, and Councillors and municipal elections, and two cloaks and everything handsome about them—so far, at least, as nomenclature goes. It is only when you ask what powers are these municipal heroes to wield, wherein do they differ from the vestry- men of old? that you receive your first great shock. Mayors, A ldermen, and Councillors though they be, all that they can do is what the vestries and district boards did before. As Lord Salisbury took pains to point out, these brand-now Corpoptions are only the old

===== 0002601_18991229_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
PARISH COITNCILS.

One important measure of the session is an Act which provides that the term of office of a Parish Councillor shall be three years instead of one year, as originally provided by the Act of 1894. The Democracy does not want to exercise its sovereign rights too fre- quently. The thing is apt to become a bore. This is a significant measure. Some day the Democracy will throw away all its toys and insist on real horses instead of rocking ones.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOP SEATS.

This thoughtful measure is associated in one's mind with a characteristic ebullition of the Prime Minister's. Lord Salisbury occasionally does some of his thinking out loud in the House of Lords, and always with start- ing consequences. However, Prime Minister though he be, he had to give way, and now on and after New Year's Day seats behind the counter must be provided, under pecuniary penalties, in all shops or rooms where goods are retailed, for the use of female assistants in the proportion of not less than one seat to every three female assistants in each room.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
SMALL HOUSES.

This is Mr. Chamberlain's measure whereby he seeks to keep faith with the nation, and to redeem his Social Programme. It is a perfectly innocent Act, warranted to alarm nobody. There is nothing compulsory about it, and it helps those who are already well able to help themselves, and it does what Building Societies for the last half century have done in tens of thousands of cases.

, Under the provisions of this beneficent .act of Parliament anybody who is actually resident or intends to reside in a house already built which is not worth £4Ol, may apply to the local authority of the area within which the house is situate for a loan, which in no case can exceed £3OO. The local authority may entertain the application or not as it chooses; but if it does entertain the application, it must see to it that the value of the house is sulficient, that the title is good, that the house is sanitary and in good repair, and that the borrower means to go on living in it. The borrower cannot get rid of the houSe without the permission of the local authority, who, at any time, may enter the premises and satisfy itself that all is well within. The interest on the loan is matter of agreement subject to this, that it must not be more than 10s. above the rate at which the local authority can at the time borrow money from the Public Works Loan Commis. sioners. Needless to add that, if default is made in any of the repayments, the house can be put up for sale or taken possession of by the mortgagee in the old brutal fashion. How this Act is to lighten the lot of labour I cannot imagine. To use Mr. Chamberlain's own characteristic phraseology, the butter is not very thick on this piece of bread. But for all that, the Act may here and there enable a working man to buy his house for & few shillings cheaper than otherwise he would be able to do. FOOD AND DRUGS.

This was really a tremendous measure, con- suming a great deal of time, and provoking by far the most amusing debates of the session. The real dispute was over the question: How far is the manufacture of margarine and margarine.. cheese a lawful industry, and what are the rights of butterS and cheese as against these oleaginous compounds? Butter and cheese, properly so-called, obtain their fatty matter from milk. Margarine gets its fat from animals. So long as things are called by their proper names (and previous legislation had decided that margarine is the proper trade name for the products deriving their fat from animals), one would have thought that people might be left alone to buy what they find bast suited for their palates or their purses. But the buttermen would not hear of this let-alone policy. If they could have got their way, margarine should be coloured blue, green, or pink, so as to distinguish it from butter, instead of being coloured the old familiar yellow. Then again, a good many margarines in the market contain more than 10 per cent. of butter-fat, and, in fact, closely approxi- mate to the true glories of butter. This was shocking. Let butter be butter, and margarine margarine; but a margarine which is one. quarter butter is the worst kind of adultera- tion. To this impassioned reasoning the Government so far yielded as actually to pro- hibit the manufacture of any margarine the fat of which contains more than 10 per cent. of butter-fat. Your butter shall not be mar- garine, and, what is more, your margarine shall not be butter. You must be either Catholic or Protestant, and you cannot be allowed to dilute your Protestant errors with more than 10 per cent. of Catholic truth.

And the parson made it his text that week, and he said likewise, That a lie which is half a truth is ever the blackest of lies, That a lie which is all a lie may be met and fought with outright, But a lie which is half a truth is a harder matter to fight.

However, according to the gospel of Long, margarine may be 10 per cent. butter, but no more. What midsummer madness it all is!

The Act provides, among other things, that the Board of Agriculture may from time to time declare certain standards as to milk, cream, butter or cheese—i.e., may say, for example, bow much water may be put into milk without the milk ceasing to be milk in the opinion, of Mr. Long. Skimmed milk must, on and after New Year's Day, be put into tins bearing a label, clearly visible to the purchaser, on which the words "Skimmed Milk" must be printed in large and legible type. PRIVATE PROCEDURE (SCOTLAND).

This is a pioneer measure of some import-. ice. Its object was to cheapen and simplify the procedure for Scottish, business in what are called local matters. Public authorities, and occasionally private bodies, sometimes require to obtain Parliamentary powers in order to carry out large undertakings. It was hought a hardship, in Scotch cases, that the preliminary inquiries and arguments should be held and delivered in the Committee Booms at We-stminster instead of in bonnie Scotland. It may be doubted whether the Provosts and Bailies are more than half-hearted in this matter. They greatly enjoy themselves in the various hotels In the Northumberland-avenue, when public business has brought them, at the public expense, to Westminster. But the demand for local Inquiries has grown louder and clearer every year for the last quarter of a century. To some extent it has grown with the growing feeling in favour of a com- plete devolution of local Lusiness to an assembly of Scotsmen meeting in Scotland. The details of the Act have no interest for the general public, nor will the amount of business to which they are applicable ever be great. It is enough to say that in certain cases provisional orders are to be permitted instead of Bills, and the public inquiries when necessary are to be held in Scotland, before four Commissioners, to be selected from a parcel of names to be nominated, so far as they are not members of either House of farnament, in tne manner provided for by the Act. These Commissioners will conduct the inquiry in Scotland, and report to the Secretary for Scotland what prclor (if any) _

they think should be made. If there is no further opposition, the order will be submitted to Parliament in a Confirmation Bill. If there is furthur opposition, the matter will be referred to a Joint Committee of both Houses, who may punish unreasonable opposition by awarding costs. This is a useful measure, and may prove significant of more to come.

THE CLERICAL TITHE ACT. This hotly-contested measure occupies very little space in the Statute Book. Like all Mr. Balfour's legislative children, it is a very little one, nor is it destined to live long. It will breathe its last on March 31st, 1901. It seems hardly worth while mentioning 'so mori- bund a trifle. Whilst it lasts, it annually divides some £87,000 of public money among 10,000 of the clergy of the Church of England, in order to remedy an alleged injustice in our rating law.

MISCELLANEOUS.

Ireland hag succeeded in changing the date for shooting partridges in that distressful country. You may now begin shooting that wild-fowl on September Ist. instead of on the 20th. There has also been establiehed a De- partment of Agriculture and Technical Instruction for Ireland, which will, I hope, do much to promote peaceful industries within Irish borders.

The Finance Act was uninteresting, nor do I think there is anything else in the legisla- tive enactments of 1899 demanding notice. I propose to paste on the back of my volume of the Statutes for 1899 a label on which the words "Skimmed Milk" shall be printed in large and legible type. A. BIRRELL.

THREE WARRINGTON CHILDREN DIE FROM BURNS.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, conducted an in- quiry at the Police Station, on Friday afternoon respecting the death of three young children who died at the Warrington Infirmary on Wednesday, from the effects of severe burns. They are three distinct cases audit is rather remarkable that the vic- tims should have succumbed within a few hours of each other from the same distressing cause:l:he first case inquired into was that relating to the death of Mary O'Neil, aged six years,daughter of John Henry O'Neil, a joiner, residing at 1, Bennett-street, who died at the Infirmary at 5.45 p.m. Deceased brother says that when be and his sister saw their mother leave the house they returned and deceased got a piece of paper and commenced to play with the fire and her clothes became ignited —The jury next considered the case of Nellie Hardy, daughter of Edward Hardy, station- ary engine driver, Hatton' farm cottage, Hallam, WllO died at 11.50 p.m. on Wednesday at the Infir- mary. The mother of deceased was washing clothes at about 3.30 p.m. on the Wednesday and she had occasion to leave the house to go and draw water from the well. Immediately after she heard screams and saw deceased by the garden gate with her clothing in flames. The child told her mother that she was in the act of looking at the clothes boiling in the washing boiler and her dress became ignited by the fire.—The concluding case inquired into related to the death of Martha Ann Beddard, aged two years, daughter of Richard Beddard, general labourer, 23, Barnard- street, Warrington, who died at the Infirmary at 6.15 p.m. on Wednesday. It appears that at 1.15 p.m. on Wednesday, November 29th. deceased's mother went out to the back to hang some clothes , on the line, leaving the child and two others in the I kitchen alone. he remained out about 15 minutes but in the meantime the sister of deceased went into the house and found the child in flames.—The jury returued a verdict of "Accidental Death." but in the last named ease,the mother was reprimanded by the Coroner for leaving the children for such a length of time alone, seeing that she could have left them in charge of her eldest daughter if she had seat for her from across the road.

The annual ball in aid of tile funds of the Sacred Heart Schools took place at the Gymnasium on Tuesday evening. There were about 150 couples present. PATRIOTIC CONCERT IN PARR HALL.

The success which attended the patriotic concert in Parr Hall, Warrington, on Saturday evening was tempered by a smaller attendance than was perhaps to be anticipated at a time when patriotism is so pronounced. The proceeds were in aid of the Mayor's War Relief Fand and such a worthy object warranted a larger attendance. An excellent pro- gramme had been arranged and a most enjoy able concert was given. The leading vocalists were Miss Annie Bell (soprano), Miss Maggie Thomas (contralto), and Mr. Arthur Weber (bass). "The Swallows" (Cowen), and" II Baccio " (Arditi) were the two songs interpreted by Mies Bell but neither can be considered very suitable for a " patriotio " concert. It is, however, only fair to state that Miss Bell's singing was greatly appre- ciated, her cultured voice being heard to dia. tinct advantage in both songs. Miss Thomas's two songs were "The Minstrel Boy " and "Eileen Allanah "in both of which she gained successes. Mr. Arthur Weber has on previous occasions eetab• lished himself in the favour of Warrington audie ences and on Saturday he fully maintained the re- putation he has gained. His songs were well chosen for the occasion and found a responsive chord in his audience. Complying with, a recall for his singing of" Oar Empire's Glory" he contributed "Where are the boys of the Old :Brigade?" A new song on the Boer ultimatum also proved very popu- lar with the audience, and as an encore was demanded be gave "Rocked in the Cradle of the Deep." His concluding song was "Soldiers of the Queen" which was given with characteristic power and expression. Miss Backsheen Wood has delighted Warrington audiences on previous occasions by her tasteful and musicianly execution on the violin. Her per- formance on Saturday further enhanced heiereputa- don. She was twice encored; her interpretation of "Gipsy Dances." and "The Butterfly " being ex- tremely clever. Responding to the enthusiastic applause of the audience she played " Romance in C" composed by Mr. Leslie Harrie,who accompanied her on the piano during this piece. In his role of humorist Mr. Leslie Harris was a host in himself land kept the house in r •.rs of laughter with his humorous selection, and recitals. The eEolian glee party composed of four local vocalists sang sever. , glees, exceedingly well, the hest of the evening 1, ing "Home sweet Home." The) [Stockton Heath Ceeral Sacioty under the eonduc- torship of Mr. J. %1 Muir, A.R.C.0., also sang glees, madrigals, and car ele in a vary creditable manner. "The Banks o' doon " arranged by Mr. J. M. Muir, was especially well sung, and the audience showed their appreciation in an emphatic manner. Mrs. Wills Harper, the dramatic reciter, had been adver- tised as one of the artistes, but she was unable to attend owing to indisposition, and at the last moment her place was taken by Miss Kate Beesley. She recited " Ordered to the Front," " Kruger's New Year Party " and "The Absent-Minded Beggar" in her usually acceptable manner. , Dur- ing her enunciation of Kipling's patriotic verse a collection was taken on behalf of the Mayor's fund. The onerous duties of accompanist were discharged by Mr. W. H Payton, Mus. Bac. During an interval in the programme, the Mayor said he had been asked to suggest to the audience that a hearty vote of thinks should be accorded to e friends who had taken part in the concert whicla had been given, and it gave him great pleasure to submit a resolution to that effect, especially as the artistes were practically giving their services. A considersble portion of the printing had been done withent cost, and also the advertising. (Applause). Mr. E.L Jones who had got up the concert,had been put to a great deal of trouble, and he should be in- cluded in that comprehensive vote of thanks. (Hear bear.) That was the second concert which had been held during the week fear a similar purpose— for the help of the wives and dependents of rever- lets ; and while suhniitting the vote of thanks he should like to include in it the name of Mr. Percy Randall, who got up the concert held a few nights ego, and which was the means of raising a con- siderable sum of money. When he told them that at the present time there were 197 dependents en the fund they would agree with him that they would nee4 all the money that could be raised be- fore the war was over. It was their duty as well as their pleasure to acknowledge and encourage these voluntary efforts. (Applause.) The resolution was put to the meeting and heartily agreed to.
