WARRINGTON.

• THE organisation of the Criticism of the ;2 W War Office. ar Office is sure to he %ex- posed to a large amouat of pungent criticism at the present time. Disaster quickens criticism, and there are so many differ- ences between theories of preparation and the actual facts,. it is, so easy to be aftervvise, it seems so necessary to say something disrespectful of somebody just now and we are all so suspicious,. because]: our sanguine forecasts have not been realised, that there is considerable danger of our being unfair alike to &Department and to persons, within and without it. Questions are being raised about mules and transport which illustrate the first point suggested. It would be regarded as impolitic and profligate to incur large expendi- ture •to make everything ready for war, store it, and provide for its immediate use the moment it was wanted. Here the War Office and the taxpayers are in agreement. Had mules been actually bought six months ago, there would have been an outcry, and the same may be said as to waggons. For years individual members of Parliament have been suggesting more horses for the cavalry and artillery, and neither the War Minister nor the public has been sympa thetic. When it has been thought judicious to store materials they have nearly always suffered in the process. A story about strks of helmets with rats eating through them, has reached us from a source which entitles it to be accepted. We know that small arm ammunition suffers by keep- ing, and we hardly suppose that any War Depart- ment in Europe keeps on hand a stock of live shells greater than if required for present use, with a small reserve to meet suddenly arising events. The power to expeditiously meet emergencies is what we have a right to demand from the War Office. But we fear that the War Office has seldom been fully alive to. the circumstances of the time, and prepared for the real strain which actual war invariably imposes. It is always caught with good plans on paper and rudimentary arrangements to carry them out. A good deal of the blame for this rests on Parliament and the country, and on the readiness with which we be- lieve in persons rather than in system. We have capacity in abundance, but it is seldom put to the best use. Whenever a crisis comes we have to rescue the army from a muddle of some sort. It is partly our own fault. Sleepinesa and credulity come over us every time we have made a small change, and it is usually so costly that we hesitate about again entering upon the path of reform. Nor can we omit frem our consideration the fact that military men are not professionally disposed to be ardent reformers, eager to adopt new inventions and masters of the business arts of organisation Some of them, however, do so well in industrial , undertakings that they ought to be alai() to perfect our military arrangements. In a won, we cannot hold the War Office to blame for more than its ignorance and its want of preparation when war was getting nearer. We may charge the Govern- ment generally with much that has made the South African campaign a series of blunders. But we must not forget that the War Office and the Government of the day are what we make them and that the country must accept some share of their responsibility for ineffective measures and imperfect organisation. It is always ready to pay more when it is being wisely advised. It will now have to become severe, attentive, and exacting.

EVRRY now and then some. Government Without Party. political prophet arises. who proclaims that we shall soon be able to dispense with government by party, as we have abandoned other hurtful methods in politics. It is pleasant to listen to these • predictions, and to dream of the coming time. But when we come to details :we discover that matters are not so pleasant, and that we may have the same thing under another name. Lord KELVIN has been declaring, in Scotland, that the next century will see the end of our party system. We are to substitute for it a perpetual coalition of eminent men, in whom the country has liontidence, and whenever the individual Ministers do badly we are to get rid of them, without such a revolu- tion as is involved in the dismissal of one Ministry and the formation of another. How such a system would work we can readily see. If the present Colonial Secretary were treated on the KELVIN plan he would •be invited to retire. Would the House of Commons vote his retire- ment just now ? And if not, be would have to remain until the electors were consulted. This would mean an election on a personal issue, and just as much turmoil as would be created by an ordinary party election, unless we endowed a Ministry with the power to get ri:l of a colleague, or placed that power in the hand of the S wereign —a still more dangerous step. Under our present plan wa have, in the main, a one-man system. The country expresses its confidenc3 in Lord Sa.rds- BLRY, say, or in somebody else, and he forms a Ministry. Is it conceivable that fifty years hence some person, then of equal note, would at once proceed to invite an eminent man from the other

===== 0002601_18991229_art0064.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
-side to join his Cabinet because he was well versed in foreign affairs, in army or navy administration, a sound financier, or an excellent maa for Lord Chancellor? Or is it conceivable that we should conseut to elcct a Cabinet by plebiscite, and that such men would be selected as were popularly ad- judged to be best for special offices and received the largest number of votes? A Government so created could not have a common policy, and there would be some friction in consequence. How would a successor be appointed? The House of Commons might ballot for a successor, or the country might have a vote upon the point. The first plan would be speedy, the second would be tedious and costly. Taere is another method. The country might give a decision on the several points of a pragrarame, and then a cabinet might be formed to carry it out. M.P.'s against the programme would become the Opposition, and government by party would return. We cannot get rid of the opposingideas which govern politics. Mr. J. S. Raz defined the two leading ideas a- s -"permanence and progression." They are at present, if somewhat roughly, the governing ideas of the Conservative and Liberal party. The old and the new are always jostling together, ani the desire, to preserve the foraier and develop the lat- ter is eternal in the human mind. In Turkey and China there is no party system, because new ideas are unwelcome and suppressed. Lord KELVIN'S -type of non-party government can hardly be de- rived from either of these States. It is a dream and our nearest approach to it will be the effort to attain a more general agreement upon all great subjects.

Pantomime appears to retain the strong posi- tion it has won for itself in the theatrical world, though its taste, usefulness and historicContinnity have all been bitterly attacked by serious minded people who are for ever in the dise:ials over what they term the prostitution of the theatre. Much of the so-called" fun" is, of course, difficult to defend. It is not so much fun as vulgarity, and it springs in part from the adoption for the time being of music-hall manners as well as music hall stars. Pantomime has also lost the old significance. It is no longer a stage presentment of a fairy tale,to which children might fairly clamour to go. Modern pantomime is intended not for the children, but to feed that demand for liberty and licence which is customary at the time of year. Only in one respect is modern pantomime linked with the old. It deals with old stories, and apparently cannot exist without them. In London, for instance, there were no less than five "Dick Whittingtons ' and three "Babes in the Wood" presented for the entertainment of Boxing Day revellers. It is not that pantomime authors suffer from paucity of invention, but that the public, dimly remembering old traditions, looks with suspicion upon new stories, and will not support them:

The experiences of the present season prove that the vogue ef sending Christmas and New Year cards is passing. There was a time, but a few years back, when each card was selected to express a particular sentiment for the pers)n to whom it was sent, and in that way the custom, as applicable to persons of all ages, seemed nothing more or less than a huge development of the valentine practice. After this, as every family increased the circle of exchanges and so found selection tiring, came the special card with an original expression of good will, indicative at least of the individuality of the sender. Next came the printed card of general pattern bought at the shops, only the name and address to be inserted at so much per hundred copies by the printer. Afterwards some few people remembered that such matter fall within the circular rates of the General Post Office, and leaving the envelope open affixed only the half- penny stamp. Other people, having many acquaintances, and wishing to keep in touch with them all, gave an address hook to a clerk, and the "circular" notion was complete. Last stage of all some public persons this year are advertising in the daily newspapers their regard for their friends as a whole. On the presumption they have no enemies this message must be taken as to mankind. Who can value such a communal display of good will Surely the money could be better spent on a practical benevolent object.

Amidst all war's alarms the twenty :or thirty thousand soldiers in General Buller's camp at Chieveley enjoyed their Christmas day right royally. The noar presence of the enemy, the re- cent defeat, the slain and wounded comrades were all temporarily forgotten in feastings and merry- makings such as only " Tommy " knows how to organise and enjoy. There was no "spectre at the feast " ; cavalry, infantry, gunners,and men of the Army Service Corps vied with each other in their gaiety and good-fellowship. Correspon- dents tell us that" many of the menus were quite elaborate, and included soups a la Colenso,salmon Ala Tugela, entrees a la Ladysmith and a la Grobler's Kloof, joint a la trek ox, John Bull puddings, and lydditte mince-pies; whilst the fruits, sweets, and wines afforded capital oppor- tunity for witticisms." We are sure that the gallant army must have been immensely benefited by the brief relaxation from arduous duty, privation, and danger. With Christian for- bearance the British gave the Boers a rest and the famous 4.7 zuwal guns ceased hurling shot and shell. The followina day saw a reversion to the more serious business of the campaign, and in order to impress more clearly on the enemy the sacredness of Christmas Day, a Christmas box of one hundred deadly shells was distributed among them. No doubt they appreciated the gift as much as they did the re- laxation of the previous twenty-four hours.

Warrington is certainly bearing its full share of the responsibilities and anxieties connected with the war in South Africa. A large number of War- rington men are already at the front and in a day or two we may expect to hear that the South Lan- cashires who had such an enthusiastic send-off from Warrington, have reinforced General Bailer in Natal. The recent embodiment of the Militia has also *drawn a number of men from Warring- ton and the decision to call but the remainder of the reservists will result in a further drain upon the resources of the town in this direction. On Friday it was announced that at least five mere policemen in the Borough had been recalled to the colours and it may be taken for granted that there are many other reservists in the town wtio will have to cease their employ- ment and answer to the call of duty in the field. There is also the possibility that the patriotic offer of our local volunteers for service in South Africa will be accepted by the War Office.. When the offer was .made in September last there seemed little possibility that the services of Volunteers would be required but a great deal has happened since then and if it is the privilege of the Volunteers to serve their country at the front we have little doubt but that they will render a good account of themselves and reflect credit on the town which has for so long been proud of its local defenders.

Up to the present the Warrington men at the front have fortunately escaped being slain. There are several, however, lying in hospital suffering from wounds, and with such a direct and personal i interest n the campaign it is only natural that the thoughts of many are turned in that direction and that their prayers are offered for the safety of their friends. It is a source of gratification that Warrington has not orilv coLtributed soldiers but also men whose duty it is to care for the sick and wounded. Last week another Warrington ambulance man departed for the front as an hospital orderly, and in this work of mercy he will have the sym- pathy of his fellow townbmen. Warrington is not only showing its loyalty by sending out men but also in remembering those who have gone and the dependents they have left behind. The ladies of Warrington are to be congratulated upon res- ponding so readily to the appeal to provide a Xmas gift of woollen clothing for the local regiment ,(the

===== 0002601_18991229_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
Ist South Lancashire) now on service in South Africa. The ideais to despatch beforethe 10th of January next parcels ot clothing, consisting of socks, Balaclava caps, and khaki jerseys—all use- ful articles—for our local soldiers and it may be safely anticipated that the presents will come as an agreeable surprise to the recipients and serve to remind them that they have not been forgotten by those at home. The Mayor's war fund for the help of reservists wives and families,we are glad to see,has reached a handsome total. Many additional cases will be included in the committee's relief list and as the war is likely to last some considerable time it is absolutely necessary that the tide of subscriptions should continue to flow and not to ebb as it shows signs of doing.

WARRINGTON RUGBY FOOTBALL CLUB. History of the Club.

Although the Warrington Football Club cannot boast of any great antiquity, especially when compared with some other clubs whose careers we have already traced, the organisation has of recent years played a very prominent part in the Rugby Union and Northern Union world. Members of the club boast that it was through Warrington that tho Northern Union was inaugurated. Mr. J. E. Warren, the late secretary of the club, was really the pioneer of the new union, and. upon his retire- ment last season he was presented with a gold watch by the Northern Union, after the last match between Lancadaire and Yorkshire, as a token of the high appreciation in _ which he is held by Rugby footballers in general and members of the Norteern Union in particular. During the club's association with the Northern Union, which of course dates from the commencement of the latter, its greatest feat has been reaching the semi-final stage of the Northern Union Cup competition in the first year of its inauguration. On that occasion they were defeated by Batley at Huddersfield, on a miserable day for football. As regards the Lancashire competition, Warrington have always occupied about the fifth or sixth pesition. Prior to this season the club always could boast of playing an entirely locally-born fifteen. but, with the migration of the junior players to other clubs in the Lancashire district, the committee have been obliged to go outside the town for players, with the result that the condition of the club at the present time is far in advance of that which it has occupied for the last three seasons. Before passing to the early history of football in Warrington, it should be added that the town club in MEI won the Lancashire and Border Towns' Cup in the first year of its inauguration. At present the club boasts of a mrm- berahip of about five hundred, and is in a most flourish- ing condition. So much for the recent history of foot- ball in Warrington, which is too well known to need of further mention here. The early career of Rugby foot- ball in the town is not so well remembered, although probably of even greater interest. EARLY HISTORY.

The Warrington Football Club was formed in the year 1875, bat never attained any ereat proficiency until an amalgamation was arranged with the Padgate Excelsior Club, a young but rising club, which for some time was looked down upon by the premier organisations. Ulti- mately, however, matches were arranged between the rival clubs, which resulted in a decisive victory for the juniors. The Warrington Club were so discredited by their defeat that they wereklad to treat with their rivals, and as the funds of both clubs were about as low as they could be the terms of amalgamation were easily settled. Tho majority of the most prominent players of the now defunct Latchford Club also threw in their lot wila the cembined teams, and this may be said to have been the stepping-stone to the conspinious position since filled by Warrington. But the real commencement of their prosperity dates from 1884, when the Warring- ton Wanderers and S. Elphin'e Clubs joined hands, owing to the difficulsy of these organisations in securing suitable fields. The infusion of so much new blood into the team soon had a beneficial result, victory after victory crowning their efforts, and at the end of their first searon they had won seventeen matches out of the twenty-four played, lest but two, while five had been drawn. In addition the financial standing of the club improved, and their supporters increased in numbers. Negotiations, too, were entered into for a new enclosure and for the past ten years or so they have possessed one of the best Rugby football grounds in tha country. The doings of the club for the eight seasons prior to the formation of the Northern Union were as follows :

Season. Played. Won. Lost. Drawn. 1887-38 1888-89 1889-90 1899-91 - - "... 1891-92 1891-93 ...... 35 1893-94 ...... 42 ..... 30 1894-95 ..... 38 THE NORTHERN UNION.

Angust, 1895, the Warrington committee decided to join with twenty-one other club; in Lancashire and Yorkshire in forming the Union which was afterwards called the Northern Rugby Football Union. An unfor tunate dispute with the first team of players almost at the commencement of the sason deprived the club of ten of the usual players, and had much to do with the partial non-success of the team before Christmas. Up to that time only five matches had been won, but with the New Year 'came the turn of the tide. Experience began to have its effect on the team, and of the twenty- three remaining matches only eight were lost ; the final result being, forty-two matches played. seventeen won, twenty lost, and five drawn. Tak'ng into con- sideratinn the quality of the clubs which were engaged—the most formidable list of fixtures the War- rington Club has ever had to face—this result was were favourable. Last season Warrington played thirty-eix matches, winning seventeen, loing eighteen, an 4 draw- ing one, as compared with sevonteen Tyne, corent,,rn lest, and two drawn for the season'lB97-93. In the League table they again figured ninth with twenty-three. points against twenty-tw, for the previous season. THE PLAYERS.

Many county players have been selected from the ranks of the Warrington CTui, perhaps the most notable of which is the present eapt,in. J Bate, better known as "Smack." Bo cow, the custodian, played under Rugby Union roles for the County Palatine ; while Barber, who was injured at Oldham two years ago, also earned honours under the Northern Union rules. Mirrison and Dakin,of the forwards, are also players who have greltly distinguished themselves. Of the old players the most famous is " Fair" Turnr. He nap- tamed the club in 1884 and again in 1890. In 1887 he was rewarded with county honours, and for many seasons he was one of the most brilliant forwards in Lancashire. Respenting his scoring powers, he held the lead in the forwards for a number of seasons, while in one match he scored the large number of nine tries. Turner, in his day, was one of the best all-round athletes in the North of England. In the competition promoted by the National Physical Recreatioa Society he won the champion shield fer physique. As a swimmer he was much above the average. Few men have done as much for Rugby football in Warrington as has Mr J E Warren, the late secretary. A capable athlete.' be assisted the defunct Padgate Club before it amalgama- ted wish Warrington. He was then elected captain of the latter organisation, and as a criterion of his pray it may be said that he scored more tries during the reason than all the other players together, his position being half back. The present secretary is Mr H Ashton, also an old player and captain of Warrington.—From the " Golden Penny."

SUICIDE AT RUNCORN.

A Sad Christmas Spectacle.

On Christmas morning, the residents of Runcorn received a rude shock on learning that Thomas Lambert, a respected lock-keeper in the emptyv of the Manches- ter Ship Canal Company, had committed suicide by hanging himself upon his return from work that day. On Wednesday morning, Mr. A. E. White (depnty coroner) held an inquest upon the body, at the Runcora Police Court.—Mr. Robert Wright was foreman of the jury. Louisa Reylance, wife of Eiward Roylance, stated she resided at 14, Picow-street, Runcorn. Tnomas Lam- bert (the deceased) was her father and resided with her. He was 66 years of age, on the 20th of October last,and was a lock-tender on the Manchester Snip Canal. She last saw her father alive on Meaday :morning at six o'cleck. He had boin werkine nights, and came home and washed himself as usual in the back kitchen. She did not see anything more of her father, nor bad she seen the body. Between 6.30 and 7 o'clock she calle 1 t o her son beeause she felt uneasy. Something seemed to tell her there was something wrong. Deceassd was going to be married, but some time last week he stated he did not know how he would get on as the woman appeared to be a shiftless sort of woman. He also told witness there was a son' and he did not like the idea of keeping him if he kept the old woman. fie had asked his intended wife about the lad, and she had told him he was going away immediately. Deceased had arranged to be married on Tuesday. Thom as oylance.eas-stove fitter Gebbon's-read ,Stret- ford, London, described deceased as "his grandfather be marriage "—his stop-mother being the previous witness Upon his step-mother calling to him he went to call "father " and look for the deceased. He ceuld not find him, but saw what he took to be a tall strange man in the closet. Receiving no answer from the person in the closet he returned to the house and his father coming in shortly afterwards he told him about it. Edward Roylance, in the employ of the Menchester Ship Canal, said deaeased was his wife's father. He sew the deceased hanging in the-closet. Teat was after the son had told him that he "could not find father, as he had gone out; but there wee a big men standing at the bottom of the yard." Oa going there he found a I man hanging in the closet, and ran out of the bou.e all , the way to the Police Station. Oa arriving at the Police; , Station he told the. officers in charge and was immedi- ately accompanied by a sergeant and a constable. They cut the deceased down. The time wnuld be about eight o'clock.. He was dead then.—The Coroner*: Suppose you had cut him down when first you got there. There is a possibility he piglet not have been dead ?—No re- ply.-you have no reason for not cutting him down only that you had no nerve P—Witness : That's se.— The Coroner : But you know it was your duty to do so.—Witness: I ceuld not do it. Sergeant William Brewster, stationed at Runcorn, said he was told by the last witness, who was very nervous and excited, that his father-in-law was hanging in the closet. He went there and found him suspended by a rope from a rafter. The rope had been wound twice round the rafter and made secure. Then, he would suggest, the deceased had got on the closet seat and putting the noose ralsnd his neck stepped off. His feet were only a little distance off the floor. The man was to all appearance dead when Cut down, hut witness and Constable Nicholls tried artificial respiration until the arrival of Dr. McKenzie • who pronounced life ex- tinct. Wituess found nothing upon the decea ;eel in the shape of written matter explainine the cause for the action. There was 15s. 2d: in a purse, a gold wedding ring. 4 keys, a pocket knife, and a silver watch and chain, found on the body. The last witness had told him that he was too rfoich upset to cut the body , down.

Sergeant Brewstcr remarked that ther? were a good many people under the impreesion that they were not to cat a body dowOlotil the arrival of the police. The Coroner, in 'summing up, said it did FA,ckms unfortunate, that the son-in-law was so overcome t,y hi nervous fears, that he left the unfortunate man hanging for nearly an hour. It was quite possible, had he been cut down, his life would have been saved. Still it was not for him to blame the witness, as It was not case of misapprehension as regarded his obligation to cut the body down. He was simply unable tod) so on account of his nervous fears. He (the Coroner) wished however, to take that opportunity of emphasising the fact that it was as mull the duty of any person to cut down a body as it was for a person to attempt the rescue of a drowning person. [BY A CORRESPONDENT:]

At last Run-corn hs awakened to its duty to its "reservists " and a real scene of enthusinsm was witne.sel for the first time on Wednesday last But even thi4 might not have come about but for the initiati7e,of the "Boys Brigade" from Halton. Halton has long been d.pised by the Runcornian, as a place from which nothing great could spring; but once again the " humble village " has set the "proud town" an example. However, it is gratify- ing to see it is being honestly followed.

The Deputy Coroner's remarks regarding the "cutting down of bodies "should of course, receive their due weight, and make people more careful in future. Still the" misapprehension" spoken about is not so universal as supposed. Indeed it is said to have originated in the inventive brain of a deci- dedly nervous man, whose tremors rendered him incapable of giving "first aid."

That the Coroner did not censure the witness is a matter for congratulation, for too often a nervous man has been censured for not rescuing a drowning man, and has even occasionally been dubbed a coward, though his conduct has been due to nothing but an unfortunate nervousinem In mat- ters of this kind there is too marked a trace of the hand of fate, to blame a fellow creature for a neglected opportunity.

I hear that the loud trumoetings respecting the advent of the" Ratepayers' Association" are likely to remain vociferation and verbosity. The talk, too, of a "Tradesmen's Aksociation" is also becoming fainter and fainter, and there appears a definite prospect of remaining just where we are.

Nonconformists should wake up if they desire their Free Church Council to become a living, ns e- ful, defensive or aggressive, institution. The sup- port extended to it in Runcorn is, to judge by that accorded to its lectures, half-hearted, lackadaisical, grid utterly unworthy of the Nonconformist com- munity of the town. It is absurd to look at the prospects of the Council in anything but the true light, and tailing a better appreciation of the com- munity responsible for its existence, it will undoubtedly become so far as Runcorn is concerned a " Dead Letter."

Ones again rumours are afbat that the fate of one of the Runcorn works is being" weighed in the balance" and if found wanting will be closed. My authority for the statement is reliable, but desires that the public may be gently warned of the ap- proaching loss, so that,should the worst be realised the blow may not be so severe, Should the powers that be, decide upon a further renture,and continue the works, my pen shall advise the public of the fact with far greater pleasure than this is re corded.

Seeing him off by the 12.50 train were his wife and child, •Serzeant Clays, and his other friends of the Frodsham police, Mr. Youd and others. Gis chief has intimated that his "job" will be keptopen for him.

A Warrington Postman with Methuen's'

Private H. R09C003, of the Royal Engineers, who pre- vious to the war was a postman at Warrington, and lived with his parents at Besevick.street, has written an .interesting letter home from Orange Itiver. The com- munication is dated December 3rd, and he states that bets still safe and sound. He is now in a good job 30 Miles from the enemv.,ennployel in the office of Major S'ewart, who is the director of railways. The writer points Out that they have a saloon carriage fitted up as an office. At night eight of them sleep in it, whicl, he says, "is much better than sleeping on the bare ground with the canopy of heaven for a roof,as the infantry and cavalry have to do." "'After a week's hard fighting," he con-- tinues, " The troops on thia border are having a rest, and they need it lean assure yon For instance the other day our fellows at the litttle of Modder River fought for twelve solid hours and that upon one bisauit a man given out in the Early morning. Some of them were lying f see downwards for nearly the whole time firing incessantly. Of course the engineers do some most valu- able wode and affect not a little the sneers,' of a battle but for sheer bard soldiering the infantry cavalry and artillery are the boys' to catch it." In concluding his letter Private Roscoe anys : " tarn of opinion that this job will last five or six months Yet. Of ceurse lam only guessing, for it may be less or it may be more."

AFTER THE ULTIMATUM.

(BY Ma. 11. WHITHAM).

Since I wrote last, the unfortunate business in the Transvaal has taken shape, and the Boer in very boorish fashion has taken a header the re- SIMS of which he may not find satisfactory. /t is now usele,s to attach blame to anyene, or to m Lke any hypercritical remarks, the work must be seen through. Seeing that two, possibly three weeks must elapse befpre these lines are in print, I need make uo remarks on the conduct of the roaming forces.

The suspense of the past few weeks has been terrible, nod, if what I see in my paper each morning be true, the anxiety of the Britisher wherever he might be, at home or abroad, has been of the keenest. I see from this morning's news that a state of war has been declared, and that British diplomatic agents are giving notice to that effect to the various governments. This of course does away with the etymological feature of the question. It does some- thing more, and it is of this I wish to speak. It places friendly, or neutral nations in the clear po4- tion of having to keep an eye on what is going on within their own borders. It also makes it possi- ble for intervention by other powers to stay the waste of life and treasure. That the latter may not be needed we all hope; that the former is neces- sary gnes without saying to those who note what is going on in Kurope and America. Reading the war reports day by day one is struck with the large amount of sympathy, se celled sympathy, given to the Boars by the French and Germans. It is not wanting also in Russia, and allow me to say this sympathy is very boisterous in America, especially is this so. out here in the West. Toe three first nations declare they are friendly, this may be so. We are proud to believe it really is so with the 'United States Gevernment, and, with something more than the 'government. The intelli- gence, the deep intellectual life of America, tee sober, and the histerical spirit of the nation is with England in her struggle with "our brother Beer.' Then wy this sympathy for this Boar? It would puzzle ths wisest head among U 3 to account for hny of the actioas, or positions France may se fit to take. Since she first lost the Royal isliots,who with all the piivileges of Divine rights squeezed the brain out, of the people, and welcomed the Corsican Se t Hee she has never known peace within her bor- ders, or known hove to trust the nation that is not only her best customer, but whose people are most likely to offer the hand of friendship and love, even though they be citizens of" Perfidious Albion." -It is not out of place to ask the question. Is it sym- pathy forethe Boers or is it the internal enemies of France, and of peace and order wherever it may be found who keep alive these bitter feelings? I think it is, and Rochefort and his crew night well occupy the attention of the French people. And what of Germany? Have the Germans any- thing to gain, or has the civilizing spirit of the world anethino to gain from the defeat of the British and the victory of the Beers ? In Germany Focialism grows fast and why? Is it because her people are happy and contented ? Is it because her Emperor tries year after year, and succeeds in a measure in preventing her workmen from conbin- ing for ;heir own protection, and from seeking such increase in wages as they may think desirable, or is it because he thinks, and makes his thoughts practical that the German workman should live as contentedly as he can the lire to which God has called him, and it he cannot live, to die and be done with it, and make room for a tougher man? No, I don't believe it is any one of these things that is sending German officers into the Transvaal. It is the same unquiet, restlees spirit that we have always seen exhibited at the International Trade Conference, The spirit of opposition and of jeal- ousy. Again the question, has Germany anything to offer the world in place of the British Empire? With Russia it is useless to deal. A nation that is scarcely ahead of either China and Japan, in re- spect toliberal ideas, is scarcely likely to offer, the world's workers anything they would even stop te ,consider.

The press of these nations, that is,*the Rochefort press, in whatever nation it may be found has a fling at Egypt, or may be India, or say the Soudan. If these people would only read more, they would talk less, and perhaps they weuld find that the world owed England something after all. ' And now, what of my own adopted country? wish to say nothing, nay, I feel nothing of anxiety as to what America will do should England's course be blocked. It was wise foresight that directed Enc,rland's course durinethe America-Spanish War. It seems mean to say what would have happened had her coarse been chalked out according to Con- tinental ideas, but all the same, the European Con- tiaental Courts will be a long time before they for- get the significant attitude taken by British ships in the Bay of Manilla. The true and honest American citizen will not 'forget it either. It is the other fellow I baust speak of not the true and honest citizen, but the fellow who can he neither honest to his native lend, nor yet to the land of his adoption. We have in this city a priest,Fether Yorke byname. This holy Father has bad a year's holiday during which he visited his native land, Ireland. In a spisech he made on his return, be says—" I was a stranger in a strange land, I found the people, lam sorry to say, happy and prosperous. Why do I say I am sorry? Because it will make them content with British rule." This man is a great and a shin- ing light among the Irish Catholioabut not, I think, among the native American Catholics. He is really the stormy petrel of the religious, and political world of this city. His lectures and speeches are numerous, an I he rarely misses a chance of stirr- ing up the race hatred, we ought to have left be- hind us when we crossed the Atlantie. This Irish spirit of hatred towards the British, leads a mayor of Cork and of Limerick to pray for God's help for "our brother Boer." It does something more. It calls mass meetings in our largest hells, and crams them full from ceiling to floor, and gives the poor benighted Irishman. and German an opportunity-to talk of organizing regiments, nay armies to go help Uncle Paul. The Irishman seams to yell with de• light when an opportunity offers him to dance a jig on the tail of John Bull's coat. And yet what has he to offer the world ? Throughout America the Irish politiciaa is a curse. li-ep him out of pelFicson Iha is" ova ias you or I." I work with the Irishmen, and he is not a bad fellow to work with, but I prefer not to work under him With the ,following excerpt from the "New York Times" my readers may fairly gauge the American attitude. "But the main source of comfort that Lord Salis- bury finds in a situation which certainly needs all the comfort which can be brought to it, is the sym- pathy and support of Germany and the United States. Perhaps it would be more accurate to say, in the support of the Government of Germany and of the people of the United States. For the public opinion of G?rmany, to judge it by its recognised organs, is no more friendly to England then the public opinion of France. It is only the German Government which sees its account in favouring good relations with England. In this country, on the other band, there is undoubtedly a preponder- ance of sytiopathy with England. This sympathy is in great part due to the attitude of Great Bri- tain during the war with Spain, so distinctly in op- position to the attitude of Europe. but it is also and still more largely due t,o the perception that the cause of Great Britain is the cause of modern civilisz- atm And this perception may coexist with the perception that official procedures of Greet Britain, which have not been under the immediate direction of Lord galisbury, were calculated to put her super- ficially in the wrong in a matter in which she was fundamentaily in the right. At any rate we are with whoever is trying to advance and modernise any backward portion of the world. Great Britain has done more toward the fulfilment of that pur- pose than all the other nations of the earth com- bined. And that is her real and ultimate mission in South Africa." San Francisco, Nov. 28, 1899:

I RESIGNATION OF %WOG= WYNNE FFOULKES.

Biographical Sketch. His Honour Judge Wynne Ffunit, of Chester, has tendered his resignation to the Lord Chancellor after 25 year's service. His circuit was number seven and included Wariing! •r; end many other places in Lancashire and Cheshire. It is understood that his resignation has been accepted as from the 31st inst. By his painstaking work as a Judge he nag gained the respect and confidence of all in his circuit and in his retirement he will carry with him the wishes that be will long be spared co enjoy a well-earned rein; from his arduous labours. His Honour William Wynne Ffoulkes, is the fourth son of the late John Powell Ffoulkes of Eriviat Hall, County Denbigh, his mither being the secend daughter and co-heiress of Captain Robert Jocelyn, of 6tanstead Bury, Harts, and was born on the 14th July, 1821. His early education was im- parted to him at Shrewsbury School, where' he g tined his earliest laurels, and from weich he won a scholarship at Jesus College, Oxford, to which he proceeded in due course, taking hie B. A. in 1844, and his master's degree in 1817. From Oxford he entered at Lincoln's Inn, by wl:ich inn he was called to the Bar in November, 1817, going there- after to the North Wales and Che-ter Circuit. His father's connection and his own Univer-ity reputa- tion helped to win him briefs, which his untiring energy, his courteous manner, combined with his sound knowledge of law, gradually increased until Wynne-Ffoulkes had quite a geed practice. On the 7th of Se”tember, 1854, he married Ifise Eliza. beth Benedica, the sixth daughter of the Rev. Dr. Howard. Canon of Banger. Alter a brief span of well-nigh unalloyed hepoiness, this lady died on the 28th Mitch, 1855 From this terrible blow it took Alm Wyone Ffeulkes long to recover. On the 13th SeWember, 1859, he mar- ried Miss Hester Mary, third daughter of the Rev. George. Heywood, rector of Ideford, Devon. In tba meanwhile his practice was increasing, leading to his being nominated a revi;ing barrlster in 1868, and a commissioner of esdheat. Cheseer and North Wales, the duties of which offiee he admirably per- formed from 1872 to 1874 In the following year 111 received a well merited promotion as County Court judge o? circuit number seven. Tee aopeiut- ment wie universally recogni eel as a fitting and proper one. Ever since then his Honour has held the sceles:of justice evenly in the various clung constituting his circuit. Hi, keen knowledge of commercial law has rendered his court of very great service to suitors, whilst his unsparing wrath on bogus and fraudulent actions of all sorts has ren- dered him a terror to evil-doers. His kindnesses are many, his wmerosity unostentatious but wide, and to his own profes;ion, ho is over ready to ex- tend the right hand of fellowship.

ASSAULT ON RUNCORN POLICE OFFICERS. On Wednesday at Runcorn Police Court before Messrs. A. R. Norman, S Taylor and F Wiswsll, a Norwegian sailor named John Johns of the barque ' Puritan "was charged with being drunk and disorderly on the night of Christmas day ; and further with assaulting PC, Breeze and Inspector Rieke. About nine o'clock P.C. Breeze's attentien was drawn to the Masonic Hotel by a noise and the use of foul oaths. Be found that the prisoner was the cause, having been ejected from the betef for disorderly conduct. The Constable persuaded the prisoner to go quietly away but a few minutes later discovered him repeating his conduct at the entrance to the Sailors Home in Regent-street, and as all the persuasive arts of the Constable could not appease his wrath for being ags, sin ejected, he took him into custady. On the way to the Police Station the prisoner broke away, but the constable re- captured him in High-street where the prisoner was most violent, striking the Constable on the face and butting him with his head many times. Inspector Hicks raceiesd information of the occurrence from a young lady and went to his subordinate's aid through a crowd of several hundred p^ople. The prisoner butted the laspector many timee and also twisted his thumb in such a manner, that the free use of embrocation and cold water bandages had not yet rettorei it to its normal condilon —The prisoner admitted that he was drunk but pro- fessed entire ignorance of the assaults. He was fined 54 and costs, or 7 days for the drunkenness, and was sen- tenced to ten days hard labour for each assault, the lentence to run consecutivelr. A RUNCORN MOTHER AND HE& CHILDREN. At Runcorn Police Court on Wedeelday before Meters A. R. Notman, S. Taylor, and F. Wiswall, Mary Ann Baden, a young woman, wife of a tanner, livicg in Taylor's-row was brought up on remand charged wlted cruelly illtreaaine her three children.—P.S. White said on Monday the 18th inst.,the prisoaer came to the, police office at 7 p.m. She had a razor in her poression, the blade being smeared with lei.oed- She said she hed come to give herself up in order to prevent her doing any- thing to herself and the young baby she carried. Wit- ness then went to the prisoner's house and found Willie, aged two years, had a cut on his cheek which was bleed- ing. The prisoner was under the influence of drink.— John Boden deposed that the prisoner was his wife. They had been married eight years, and lived comfort- ably together for six mouths. Then she started drink- ing. It was bad company which had caused this. Be bad often gone borne and found his wire drunk and the children neglected and no tea ready. He had found 27 pawntickets in the house relating to shoes, bedding, and clothing which the children should he wearing. He worked at Reynolds' tanyard and had given his wife an average of 245. per week. Be bad taken care of the three months old baby during the pest week,and he had had no sleep with it. He would willingly forgive the prisoner if the magistrates would do so.—Alice Boden. aged eight years stated that on the Mcnday in question her mother placed her on her knee and threatened to cut her throat with a razor. The razor was closed and she drew it across her throat. She also threatened to cut the throats of her sister Millie and her brother Willie. She cut Willie's face with the butt end of the razor. They all screamed. Her mother then left the house.— P.C. Wilson said the prisoner made the following state- ment to him in the Parade Room at the Police Station :—" I left home about five o'clock and went to the Manchester beerhouse anti. had a glass! of beer. I drank it at one draught. I had the razoV with me then. I came out and intended to use it on myself and the baby and I shall do it yet. Mr husband has been listening to some lies told by Mrs. Flnughton. She told him I had been drinkiee with Mrs. Haves and my husband believes it. It has upset me. It is all lies,"—The Prisoner said she was upset but she never intended to use the razor. She denied that she cut her boy. The blood on the rant' was caused by cutting a gathering: The meek on the boy's face was caused by a burn sometime R2O. She was very sorry for what bad happened. In reply to the Bench Boden said he was willing to give his wife another chance. He thought she would be better if the magis- trates would give her a cbance.—The Bench char- acterised the case as a very serious one, and one which was the result of drink arid advised her to keep from drink altogether. The prisoner was then bound over to come up for judgment when called upon.

WARRINGTON.

Imperial Circus Queen-Street. . The Impetial Cirens, Queen-street, has during the past week, been occupied by an exceoent variety company under the direction of Mr. James Fioni. A most interesting pro9.ramme is submitted, one of the best items being the trapez;ng performance by Nana, Nano and Nana, "the aerial wonders" Gasnier,"the mighty man eF steel and iron muscle" gives astounding weight lifting feats and the Bo- hemian,' mumieel eccentrics, provide an excellent "turn." Spriggs and Grundy coloured comedians and expert dancers, create much amusement with their contributions.and the songs of Alfred Sellers, eccentric comediansand Miss Bessie Willows are well received. The Andys, give a praiseworthy acroba- 'sic performance, and an exhibition on the rings is given by the Leonzas. The proceedings are en- livened by several clowns, the principal one being "Comical Anderson." Edison's invention, the Mic- rophone, which reproduces songs, &c, is another popular item. On Christmas Day, two sacred en- tertainments were held and were well attended. Afternoon performances took place on Boxing Day and Thursday, and one will be held to-day

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, DECEMBER 29, 1899

THE FREE CHURCHES.

"lAN MACLAREN " AND CANON HAMMOND.

The epistolary controversy between Dr. John Watson ( " lan Maclaren " ) and Canon Ham- mond concerning the doctrine of Apostolical Succession has excited considerable interest among Nonconformists. Dr. Watson, in a sermon at the City Temple, London, in con- nection with the Metropolitan Free Church Federation, spoke very plainly about a theory of orders which made the ministry of some ignorant, debased Brazilian priest " valid," ai.3 denied this quality to the ministry of Bunyan, or Dale, or Spurgeon. Canon Hammond practically admits the impeachment. Ho points out that the sons of Eli—Hophni, and Phineas—were wicked, yet the efficacy of their priestly ministrations was not thereby impaired, and that Judas Iscariot, though the Lord knew that he was to prove himself a traitor, was an Apostle. Dr. Watson retorts by answering whether Canon Hammond con- tends that Our Lord was indifferent to per- sonal character in His apostles. The corre- spondence has been printed in full in the British Weekly. The controversy is conducted on both sides in a fine spirit, with courtesy and calmness.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. PARKER AND THE WAR.

Dr. Parker has taken practically the same line on the war as Mr. Frederick Greenwood. The Boers, he says, have shown themselves formidable enemies. `Let us offer to discuss terms of pezice with them, fully resolved that if they prove obstreperous we shall continue the fight to the inevitable end. This was Dr. Parker's advice in the Daily News. The minister of the City. Temple is continually surprising and dazzling people. He has followed his article by a statement from the pulpit that he thinks the Queen in an unofficial capacity—" as a woman, a mother, a widow " could stop the war. Flow, he does not explain. "She herself must spontaneously determine. Let us leave it to the instinct and the impulse of her great motherly heart whioh has never. failed her."

THE SUCCESSOR OF THE REV. J. H. SHAKE-

SI'EARE, M.A.

As most readers of this columrn. know, the Roy. J. H. Shakespeare, M.A., before he became the Secretary of the Baptist Union about a yoar ago, was minister of St. Mary's Church, N orwich, a historic pulpit of Nonconformity. It was no easy thing to find a suitable successor to Mr. Shakespeare, but the church has hit upon a good and true man in the Rev, T. B.A:, of Kettering. Mr. Phillips is one of the coming men—if he be not " arrived " —of the Baptist denomination. He has had distinguished success at Kettering, and his people are very sorry to part with him. Mr. Phillips is an enthusiast for the Free Church Federa- tion movement, and very few men have done more downright hard work on its behalf. •

THE SHEFFIELD MEETINGS OF THE NATIONAL

FREE CIIITRCH COUNCIL.

The programme for these meetings, which are to be held from March 12 to March 15, inclusive, has now been fixed. We observe that three hours have been set apart for the discussion of public questions. There is not likely to be any lack of matter for debate. Perhaps it is unprecedented that a member of the State Church, Mr. Walter Walsh, author of "The Secret History of the Oxford Movement," should take part in the proceedings. Mr. Walsh, along with Dr. Barrett, is going to dis- cuss "The present aspect of the Protestant Movement." The great enterprise of the Free Churches, for 1901 is to be a united simultaneous mission, and the best means of making this a success will have to be considered. In view of the concentration of temperance reformers on Lord Peel's report, the discussion on "The attitude- of the Free Churches towards temper- ance legislation," which is to be opened by the Rev. C. F. Aked, the energetic Liverpool Baptist, will be of special interest and im- portance. Owing to the increasingly large attendance of representative members, the number of personal members will be limited, and application should be made at once to the Rev. Thomas Law, Memorial Hall, London.

MRS. GEORGE CADBURY,

the wife of the great "Cocoa King" of Bir- mingham, is an energetic and Christian worker, and almost as well known in the Midlands as her husband. She is very wi.sje in her sympa- thies. For some years she was President of the Birmingham "Union of Girls' Club," which represents all denominations. At the Manor, Northfield, the beautiful home of Mr. and Mrs. George Cadbury, fwo lady gardeners are employed. Some interesting details of Mrs. Cadbury's work is given in the January Free Churchman. WOrall A GUINEA A BOX:"

Mr. Joseph Beecham, J.P., the new Mayor of St. Helens, and the chief proprietor of" Beecham's Pills," is a Congregationalist, and is the subject of an article in the January Free Church Chronicle, one of the series on "Free Church- men in 3.luni:ipal Life." A long review of the Rev. J. S. Drummond's life of Dr. Burry is an interesting feature of the new number. The writer has some new information: On the second Sunday of Dr. Berry's ministrations at Plymouth Church, Brooklyn, while he was in the middle of his strmon, the church suddenly filkol with smoke, and the people became alarmed. Dr. Beiry, with the greatest eang fr!)id, summoned the assistant pastor, told him to go and find out what was the matter, and proCewled quite calmly with his discourse. He ri,-etLed the attention :of the people on himself. When the assistant came back he reported that something had gone wrong with the heat- ing apparatus, but there was no cause vhatever for alarm. Dr. Berry communicated this information to the , congregation, and the ;:ervice went on without anxiety or interruption. On Monday morning the New York papers were full of stirring accounts with big, bold headlhvs, of how the young divine from England had calmed an excited congregation in the most wonderful manner.

DR. HORTON •ON MARRIAGE AND GOSSIP.

Dr. Horton has been giving advice to•young women and girls. He thinks it "altogether wrong and unwholesome that girls should be taught to dwell on the thought of marriage as

the one aim and object of their life." But are they so taught? Dr. Horton is dead against gossip and tattle. Like Wordsworth, he would have no personal talk.

WARRINGTON CUP. Second Round.

WITTON ALBION V. GOLBORNE.—On Christmas Day these teams met under the above auspices, on the ground of the former, before a fair attendance. Witton won by four goals to nil. WINNINGTON PARK V. NORTHWICH VICTORIA.—Theso teams met on the ground o the fornaer in the Eecond round of the aboya competition. on Saturday afternoon. Scorn: Northwich Viet via, one goal; Winnington Park, nil.

WARRINGTON PEOPLE'S COLLEGE.

A Successful Concert in Parr Hall.

An exceedingly large audience assembled in the Parr Hall oat Thursday last week, when the children in the upper standards at People's College St,hool, gave a most enjoyable concert. The school, which is now under the headmastership of Mr. Edward Wallington, is in a most flourishing conlition, and at the present time over a thousand children receive instruction there. The Inspector in his last report says the school is carefully orgauized,and well main- tains its high reputation for discipline and in- struction. The familiar face of the late Mr. Charles Twiss, to whose exertions the school owes a great deal, for its present positim, was missing at the concert on Thursday, but a worthy successor has been found in Mr. E. Viral'ington, who for many years was secand master. In years gone by, when the children gave their annual concert, in what is now the Royal Court Theatre, the entertainments were a notable success, but in recent years, the concerts have been abandoned, probably owing to the lack of a hall, which has now been supplied by the erection of the Parr Hall. The re- commencement of the concerts was welcomed by a large section of the public, especially the parents of the scholars, and the hell on Thursday evening, was packed. Councillor John Crosfield, .T.P., wbo has long been a supporter of the school presided, and the audience included the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Roberts), and several well- known townsmen. The absence of Mr. F Monks, Mr. Long W and Mr. J J Crosfield was apologised for after which

The CHarearax, who had a cordial reception de- livered a brier speech. Be said it must be a great pleasure to all of them to see those children so well dressed, with their clean and bright faces, and to hear them sing. Some two years ago they had to mourn the loss of Mr. Twiss, who was headmaster of the echool for a great many years, and did a great deal to increase its prosperity. The committee .were very anxious as to who they should appoint to be his successor, because they were anxieus that the'school should maintain the reputation that it had held for many years. The committee decided to appoint Mr Wallington who had been second master for a great many years and he (Mr Crosfield), was happy to say that Mr Wallington, lad maintained the reputation of the school, and it continued to be tv.4 flourishing as ever it was before. (Applause.) He was not sure of the nnmber of chil- dren who were educated at the school, but he thought from eleven to twelve hundred, and evidence of the excellent instruction that they re- ceived was that ninety-eight per cent of the chil- dren passed the examination, and the Government grant amounted this year to £l,27o,'Which was the largest grant the school had ever received. He hoped that the children who received their educa- tion there. when they grew up, would be most valuable to the town of Warrington. Many young fellows who had been eduoated there, had turned Out to be•valuable citizens and amongst them he might name their worthy Mayor, Alderman P )berts. (Applause.) He had been most 'successful in busi- ness. With regard to his official career, he was a common Councillor for many years, until he became an Alderman, and now he bad reached the highest position—that of being the chief magis • t: ate of the town. He thought they would all agree with him that he was well worthy of the office. (Applause.) Speaking egain of tho position of the school, Councillor Crosfield said be believed there were only two schools in England that were equal to People's College and it was a great credit to the town that they had such a school, so ably managed, and which turned out such excellent men. He wished it every success and wished to say also that they were very grateful to Mr. Wallington for the admirable manner in which he conducted the school. (Applause) Ihe concert was then proceeded with ; the pro- gramme being of a miscellaneous description. Many of the songs were of a patriot:c character and most appropriate at the present juncture. There was a strong chorus of 350 voices, under the conductor- ship of Mr W H Payton, Mae Bac , the singing master at the school, and the manner in which the various items were given bore evidence of the painstaking care which must have been taken in training the scholars. There were three soloists, Miss Lena Dobson (soprano), Mr. George Clay (bass),and Mr. Arthur Griffiths (tenor). The program ase commencsd very appropriately with the singing of" Rule Brit innia "with much verve. followed by the national song "The Englishman." Special mention should be made of the chorus "The whistling thiel" in which whistling was introduced and proved very popular with the audience. Other choruses incluoed "God Bless tile Prince of Wales" " The King's Own" and" The Star of Bethlehem." The most popular item was undoubtedly Kipling's "Absent-minded beggar," which was capitally sung to Sullivan's setting by a number of boys, all the children joining in the chorus. This is the first time that it has been sung before a Warrington audience,and considering its reception on Thursday night, it seems prol. :We that it will become a popular tuna in Warrington. It had been announced that owing to the length of the programme, no en- cores would be allowed,but the audience were roused to such a state of enthusiasm that they practically insisted on one. end the last verse had to be re- peated. 'I ,vo of the most interesting items were the gipsy song and dance by the pupil teachers, and the fan song, by the girls, each of which had to be repeated. Great credit is due to Miss Brown under whose tuition the performers had been. Miss L Dob- son was suffering from a, cold, but nevertheless she gave a very c editable rendering of the " River of Years "and "The songs the children sing. Mr George Clay possesses a robust bass voice, and his contribution " The storm fiend," was very praise- worthy. He also sang in company with Mr Arthur Griffiths, a duet entitled "The battle eve," wnich was expressively given. Mr Arthur Griffiths'a voice was heard to great advantage in" Bonnie Mary of Argyle," and in response to a well-deserved encore he sang" The island of dreams." Before the con- clusion of the concart, - -

Mr. J. R. YouNo propwel a vote of thanks to the Chairman for presiding over what might be des- erihei as one of the most enjoyable concerts ever held in connection withTeople's College. r. JACKSON-11 OLMES seconded.

_ Councillor CROSFIELP, ill req?onse, said be had always taken an interest in the prosperity of the school, because it was one of the most valuable in- stitutions in the town.

Mr. E. WALLINGTON in thanking all who had taken any part in the concert, and the public for their attendance,said the large attendance was very gratifying to him and to the teachers, and was exceedingly encouraging to Mr. W. H. Payton, the singing master. The pro(!eedings: closed with the singing of the National Anthem.

ELECTRIC TRAMWAYS.

The application of electricity to street railroads—in other words to mere tramways—his, says " Engineer- ing," been an accomplished fact for ten years on the other aide of the Atlantic, where there are now thou- sands of miles of electric tramways, mostly operated from overhead lines from which the current is taken by a contact trolley wheel. If in this country the develop- ment of electric tramways has been slower, we have at least the advantage that our cities are not disfigured by networks of overhead trolley lines. No such objections hold good for rural districts, and alowly but surely both industrial aril agricultural districts of England are being furnished with electric intercommunication with its many attendant advantages. It may come as a surprise to many who think England behindhand in this respect, when they learn that while the tote! subscribed capital invested in this country in 1899 for public electric supply is about £17,800,000, no less than £20,800,030 is already invested la electric traction. There can be no question of the 'lumens° social benefit, particularly to the artizen population, afforded by this means. 'the problem of the houseaccording to "Elgineer- ing," is the equipment of busy city thoroughfaresewhere for obvious' reasons overhead wires arc inadmissible. To all the three possible methods: that dispenee with over- head coastruction, eiviz., by accumulators carried on the car, by use of slot conduits in the road and by use of surface contacts, objections are not wanting. Accumulators are found too heavy and too short-lived to be satisfactory. Conduit con- structions are objected to as too costly and as interfer- ing too much with the roadway ; while to surface-con- tacts there is brought the terrible indictment—worse than any against the conduit—that nobody has had experience of them. It is some years Mace Mr. J. Lowe equipped slot conduit lines im Washington and Chicago. It is now most significant that in three great capitals— New York, Berlin and Paris—the electric tramwave are being largey extended without overbrad wires. In Paris, where a tentative surface-contact Fiymce.in iir.s for about a couple ot' years been trier' with moderate success,about 48 miles are uow bAng equipped by Mr. Diatto on his surface-contact plan, which Ilas been in successful operatiost on a small scale at Tcurs.

CHRISTMAS FESTIVITIES.

RUNCORN.

Christmas. the greatest of the Christian festivals, was celebrated in the cutromary manner in Runcorn. At the Chemical manufaAurers, work was suspended on Saturday at wen, with th.i excetioa of e few opera- tions' until Wednesday. The Old Quay Works or the Ship Canal Company were also shut down for a similar period. There was a total stopp:ge at the Docks on Chr stmas Day. It was a black Christmas. The wintry aspect that had prevailed for some days, previowlv had changed a mild 'atmosphere d rain had cleared away the snow. On Christmas Eve there were numerous bands of carol singers, whose strains deserve description as sweetly harmonious, but there ev, re other group whoge themes were secular, but whose musical ability was not in proportion to the strength of their lungs. Good temper prevailed all round however, and kindly indulgenoe was meted out te "Saint and Sinner." On Christmas Day special services were held at the Catholic Chapel and at the Parish Church and Holy Trinity Church, the congrega- tions being fairly numerous. The sacred edifices had been appropriately decorated for the season. The only amusement provided was a concert arranged at the Tneatre Royal by Mr. Caddick, and this iiird.R well p aronised. The day throughout was dull and there I were occasional showers at rain. FRODSHAM.

Here Christmas came and pasied away quiety. The village, generally, presented no s;-•crw but the ordinary, but inside the many residenial abides Ctiristmas tide was fittingly kept up with feastingsj.;kcs and crackers. The tradesmen decorated their wicriows and displayed their goods to advantage for several days.—The churches held the usual services attendant upon the feast and were beautifully decorated for the occasion.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDNES.

The festive season of Christm sspassed off very quietly at Widnes. Things were almost on a par with those of last year, and but for a few gatherings of a social character the usual mirthful, seas= was devoid of in- terest. Services were held at the vmrious places of wor- ship, some ot which bad been adorned with evergreens. The football matches provided amusensent for the sport- ing fraternity. In other respEcts Christ,mas Day passed off very much as an ordinary day, a little duller per. Laps, owing tc the sh9ps being closed.

===== 0002601_18991229_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

. Typical Christmas i.veather favoured the winter holi- day in this district. SVet and snow eli continuously in the early part of Monday ,rendering the roads sloppy and very uncomfortable, but as the day were on matters improved scree% Eat, so that locomotion for pedestrians was more pleasant,and as a consequence a good number of people took advantage of the keen brain g air. Al- thoueh Christmas Eve fell on Sunday this did not deter the Earlestown band from following their usual practice of parading the district and playing selections of sacred music, until midnight. One or two choirs also turned out carol singing, but the elements were not by my means I avourable to such a task, sleet and rain falling in considerable quantities. On Chrietmas morning the heed was again early astir, and the district left unex- plored on the previous night, was visited, and the usual Christmas selections were played. There was no attraction et any kind in the shape of parties, but the usual services were held at the various placesof worship, and on the whole they were fairly well atendi d. There was a large exodus of people on Saturday most of whom took advantaee of the privilege grant3d of travelling at quarter fare rate, elaich enabled them at small cost to visit their friends and relatives at a distance. To coun- terbalance this somewhat.there was a fairly large advent of visitors, and on Boxing Day, Earlestown Station presented a very busy appearance, the ordinary bookings being very heavy. The fog which came on during the day, however, eonsiderably interfered with the running of the trains many of them being nearly an hour late, and this naturally caused some inconvenience. Something like 500 football enthusiasts accompanied the Earlestown team on Christims thy to a tydock and they fully enjoyed the entertainment provided for them, rendered more acceptable by reason of their favourites winning by 3 goals to ,nil in a quite uuexpected man- ner.

BOXING DAY.

The weather on Tuesday was a considenble improve- ment on the previous day, and altheu4h the roads were still very sloppy, the sun came out strongly and shed its genial warmth over the land. Consequeatiy a large number of people were about, and an equally large number left the town by train, bat the fog did not ren- der travelling at all comfortable'. Messrs. McCorqua- dales', the Vulcan Foundry, and Messrs T. and 'l'. BILL CLIFFE.

Special attention was paid to the Chrintmas feslivet at Hill Cliffe Baptist Chapel, the pastor, Rey. J. B. Hughes, preaching three times on themes suitable to the occasion. In the morning he dealt with the " Question of Peace," in the afterreem on " Helping the needy," aed in the evening on "The questien of thalucarna- tion," and suitable hymns were sung at each service. On Ohristams morning the ancient early morning service was held at half-oast six when the smaller schoolro)m was full to overflewing and a hearty spirit prevailed. Christmas hymns were sung and the pastor preached on "The Pilgrimage of the Shepherds," and a profitable service was enjeyed.— In the afternoon the annual seholars* treat was held when at.out 150 scholars,parants and friends partook of tea. The salt ol was decorated for the occasien. In the evening the public meeting and entertainment was held when the schoolroom was full and an interesting programme was gone through. The pastur presided and gave an encouraging address. Mr. Ernest Grounds, the secretary of the school, presented a brief but clasering report. Master Herbert V. Hughes gave an original prologue in which the school events of the year were reviewed in a humorous vie w,and it was much applauded. Recitations and songs were given by Minses Ada Grounds, Esther Moss, Jennie Fleetwood and Mies Bell and Annie Broadhurst, also by Messrs. J. J. Worrell, Ernest Grounds, John Grounds, Percy Grounds, Joe Moss and John Moss. Addresses were also delivered by Mr. W. Arnold (jun), Mr. H. Bradford and Mr. John Mow'. A dialogue was given in capital style by Miss S J Worrell and Messrs B Keaworthy, George, Septimus and Benjamin Moss, Robert Joins, Herbert Hughes, and J J Lvorrall. An amusing feature of these annual gatherings are the conundrums original and Biblical,which the pastor intersperses between the items of the programme and which are thoroughly enjeyed.

Tbia was in conse- quence of men from both regiments connected with the depot being engaged in South Africa. The usual Christmas dinner was provided, and the men opmt the ' day very quietly.

Festivities at the Workhouse.

The inmates at the Wartiagton Worknouse spent a merry Christmas despite all the drawbacks to loyousness which cling to an institution of this kind. The only' untoward incident occurred in the evening when five female inmates escapt dby scaling the Workhouse wall. Tney were arrested the following morning by the police and committ ed to prison. ; Mr. A.. 1. JOLLEY returned thanks on behalf of his col- leagues on the Board. There were many sad homes in England that day bat in spite of the trouble in tiemt of them—a trouble they were going to face, and were facing, and would come out on the top in the end—in spite of the terrible war that was causing so much misery and safferine, they must endeavour to keep up the old traditioue, and be as happy and as cheerful as they could Under these adverse circumstances. So far the proceethngs of the day had been a saccess, and he hoped they would be a succesa to the end. One word of advice, which he hoped would be taked in good part and in the spirit in whi,:h it was given. ACCIDENI TO THE REV. T. R. MATTHEWS.

We much regret to erate that the Rev. T. R. Matthews is confined to his bed as the result of an accident he received on the evening of Christmas Pay. After conducting the service in the morning in his church, he went to Newchurch on a visit to his son-in-law the Rev. E. W. Whittenbnry-Kaye. He left the Rectory at night in a cab for the pur- pose of getting the train at Glszebury for home. The roads were very slippery, and when near Glaze bury Station,and ascending the hill there, the horse slowed down to a walk. Mr. Matthews thinking the cab had stopped at the station, opened the door and stepped out When he reached the ground, he slipped and fell, and the hind wheel of the cab passed over his leg. He was removed home and Dr. Mouncey was sent for, who, on examination found that no bones were broken, but he advised Mr. Matthews to remain in bed for a few days. Be is now progressing favourably and hopes to be about again shortly. A RESERVIST IN TROUBLE.

At the Earlestown Police Station on Saturday morning, betore Mr. C. B. F. Borron, two brothers, Walter Brimelow and Thomas Brimelow, both of Earlestown, were charged with being drunk and disorderly in Earlestown the previous night. Walter Brimelow is a re=ervist attached to the let Battalion of the East Lancashire Itc2giment, and up to Friday night was employed at the Viaduct Works as a labourer. Having been sum- moned to join his regiment before the 30th irzst, he was taking full advantage of the abort period of liberty allowed him, and aceordingly fell foul of the police. The police did not press the charge, and Mr Borron taking into consideration the fact that he was going to the front to fight for his country,l , gave the brothers some good advice, and discharged them.—Walter Brimelow proceeded with his regi- ment to Woolwich to-day.—At the Newton Town Hall on Wednesday morning, before Mr Gillespie, Jol3n Flaherty, farm labourer, was charged with being drunk and disorderly and was fined 53 and costs. He, however, elected to go to gaol for 14 day?

We have received the followiag letter from Mra. Dob- son, of Blackbear Muse, Kentsford-road, whose Fiera, (Corporal J. W. Dabsoe, master butcher), is serving under Lord Methuen. The letter was written to a friend and is as fellows :—" Cape Town, South Africa, N vember 29th, 1899. Dear friends,--1 have just come down here from Kimberley, about 1,000 miles away for more previsions. The scenes that I have witnessed ilia last few days defy all description, but as Jam al- right and well myself, thank God! I will tell you some- thing about it. On Saturday, 25:h November, 1899, at 10 a.m. I was detailed to sit ich myself and party (num- bering about 30 all told, with 200 cattle) w:th Lord Methuen. To do this we had to cross a very wide stream called the Modder giver, and the cattle were that d—n stupid they wohtd not face the water ; so we roped s'x horses together, swam them across, alter fastening the rooc round one of the beasts' horns and then 11 the others followed. Ail our fellows that could not swim stuck to their tails and were pulled across ; and as I saw the poor devils in the water I was thinking what sort of a smile would fit po or Tommy's face This is comedy ; now for tragedy ! At 12 midnight the advance sounded, and we proceeded on our march to Kimberley. We numbered all toll about 6,000. Twenty ini'es from Medder River we were ordered to halt. On rtsumhog the march a very strange affair happeoed which is very nearly imisessible to deirritpe. We knew there were Bees somewhere about but did not know the ex act !re Oity. So Lord Methuen °cif red all the laegage behind, with three regiment; of ii fantry concealed amonst it. We had hardly proc,e abovo half-a-ssile when th,re arose the most deafening yell which was enough ta tirn your b`ood into lead. It anpearea that the B iers (how many I can't say, as tiiey were all over the shoo') thought to cut us off from the baggage, and upon ap- proaching it the threo regiments fired upon them, which so tole them by surprise that they turned and fled, and came right on to us. Talk about a slaught-hi ! There was blood and hair all over the place ! We tool them all ways, and which ever way they turned we let them • taste it.' What the full loss is to them I cannot ascer- tain, but they must el lost very heavily is, indepen- dent of killed and tv i Jed we took 200 prisoners. And nice heeiears some of them hoked, I can tell you ! lent a few whiskers off one or two of them, as mementoes. This engagement will he known as the battle of Gras Pen.' lam very grateful that I escaped ecot free. No snore this time, as I am in a hurry to catch the mail. Se twishieg you all a Merry Christmas—which seems all right, dorsn't it 2-1 remain by way of varietv, J.Va'.D., of the Military Society. From Corporal J. W. DOBSON, master butcher of the Army Ssrvice Corps, Kimberley."

There was a representative I gatherieg of both staffs and amongst those present were the Poetmaster (Mr J L Valley), Mr Broadhurst (chief clerk), Mr Garton (telegraph superintendent), Mr Metier (inspector of postmen), and several officials from the sub offices, &c. Advantage must be taken of this oppor• tunity to express his high sense of the ad- mirable menner in which the work had been dealt with. Although a vast increase had taken place this year it had been met cheerfully and effectively, and the zeal and carefulness displayed had been most praise- worthy. The Important work of organization and sepervision reflected credit on the controlling officers. Most fully did he recognize what had been accomplished and his best thanks were due to all. Apart fr.,m the immeliatei enjoyment arising from a re-union of this hind, ha sin- cerely hoped that the aims of the promoters might be fully realieed ; and those aims were bringing the staff more closely together, strengthening the bonds ef coed fellowship, and inspiring more confidence and broadening and widening the existing sympathies.
